ecort I - The Establishment of the Shanghai Office of the Japanese Emhassy
Freface
The Embassy Office in Shanghai was established in March, 1939. After the
dissolution of the Asia Promotion Board's (KOiIN) central office in Tokyo,
the Greater ast Asia Ministry (TN: herein called GA Ministry) was established
in its place, and the Shanghai Embassy Office became the responsible organ of
the ministry in Central China. hith the bolition of the Central China Liaiso
Eurtau of the Asia Promotion Board in Shanghai, the mbassy Office on 1
November l942 took over and continued its activities.
rom its establishment until the formal surrender of the Japanese Govern-
ment, on 15 lugust 1945, a period of approximately 2 years and 9 months, the
mbassy Office functioned under the directives of the Tokyo G Ministry and
as the highest control body for the whole of Central China. Its supervision
covered a wide sphere of activities, including administrntion, politics,
cconomics, culture and industry. Needless to say, by cooperating closely with
all branches of the Army and Navy, whose mostimportent bases were at Shenghai
and Nanking, during this period the office played an important role as an ex-
ecuting agent of Japan's imperialistic policy of aggression.
The activities, organization and character of the office will be explaine
in a separate report. First; the circumstances of the establishment of the
Embassy Office and of the GE Ministry will be brigfly and simply outlined.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.97)
CONFIDENTIAL
nless these circumstances are fully understood, it is impossible to do
ne activities, characteristics and organization of the Embassy Office. 
xamule, in order to determine correctly what the office accomplished it ia
beolutely necessary to touch upon the inside circumstances of the establish-
nt of the office.
I sons for the Lstablishment of the Shanghai bmbaasy Office.
The Shanghai Embassy Office was the highest on-the-apot administrative
gency of the GEA Ministry. Its sphere of activity was limited to Central
Thina, with the Peiping Embassy Cffice performing a similar function for North
hina. These offices were set up for the following reesons:
1. is a vanguard agency of Japanese imperialism, the Central China Liaison
3
Bureau of the Asia rromotion Board in Shanghai had made vast expenditures of
aoney without achieving any worthwhile rcsults.
2. Due to the exposure of the incompetence of Army officials, whose
arbitrariness had become extreme since the China Incident, Foreign Office of-
ficials, who had previously been supuressed by the military, now strengthened
their efforts to replace them.
3. New machinery had to be set up in anticipation of the relinquishment
of extraterritorial rights and the return of foreign settlements to China,
which was set for January 1943.
4. The China Iucident remained unsolved and with time was only becoming
more difficult of solution. Also, in vie. of various conditions at home and
broad, especially the realization that the war in the Facific would be a hard
truggle, Japan hastened to modify her China policy and speed preparations for
oEsce negotiations with Jhungking. For this purrose it was considered disad-
vantageous, as well as unwise from the viewpoint of political strategy, to
intain the Central China Liaison Fureau of the Asia romotion Board, which
as patently an agent of Japanese imperialism. Ja anese government circles
nich o. jectively grasped this situation, including high irmy, Navy and Civil
fi. lt, unanimously denanded a speedy "Change of Folicy".
Such were the activating forces behind the so-called "New Folicy Toward
bira". (Its essentials and effectiveness, together with the conflicts which
rose around it between the Jaranese military and the Shan hai Embassy Office,
ill be dealt with in a separate report).
ith the steady decline of Japan's military, governmental and economic
oower, the Asia iromotion Board, which was the local agent of Japan, lost the
urpose of its existence and steadily became more powerless. At this juncture,
ue to the four reasons outlined above - especially the difficult necessity in
hich Japan found itself of heving to effect a sweeping change in its China
policy, and also in the light of Japan's real situation, the new GEA Ministry
vas set up and under it, as agent for Central China, the Shanghai Embassy
'ffice was created.
Here it must be pointed out thet the GA Ministry, like the Asia Promotion
Board, employed active duty military personnel as civil officials. In occupied
territories it had coordinated its action with the Army and Navy in carrying out
+he imperialistic policies of Japan. On 1 November, the Government published
a speciel official gazette, effective on that date, which dealt with Army and
Navy ofiicers on active duty who were made full time officials of the GEa
inistry. By its provisions the posts of heads of departments, counsellors and
inspectors in the Ministry, and of special ambassadors, ministers, embassy
counsellors and inspectors in the various areas of Greater East Asia were opened
to the appointment of active duty Army and Navy officers. The first appointment
Were of Major Generals SHIOZihA and IhASaKI to the post of minister, and of
Navy Captain TANΛKA and Colonel ImAI to be inspectors in the Tokyo Ministry.

CONFIDENTIAL
The setting up of this system of specially appointed military peraonne
e administrative officers, which posts should have been filled wholly by
ficials, continued the past practice of the Asia Promotion Board and wae
further manifestation of Japanese imperialism. Despite the fact that milit
rhrference in administrative and political affairs had already led to dinda
ros results at other times, the military as always refusea to recognize thim
ná this stupidity brought disaster to Japan.
In the Shanghai Enbassy Office Army and Navy officers of field grade
were appointed as inspectors. de in the days of the Central China Liaison
Pureau of the Asin iromotion Poard, there was constant rivalry between them and
the Foreign Office officials, The cnly point of difference was that now they
'ad lost their power to oppress the civilian officials because of the marked
decline in the strength of the military.
3. The Shanghai Embassy Office - a non-diplomatic organization.
The Shanghni Embassy Office and the reiping Embassy Ofrice used the title
'umbassy", but in reality they were not organs of the Foreign Office but
ubor nate branches of the newly created GA Ministry, formed to oarry out
apan's new policy toward China. Accordingly, the consulates-general and the
onsulates in the principal cities of Central China were placed under the juris-
iction of the Shanghai Embassy Ofrice. Thus they lost their character as
rgans of the Foreign Office, instead becoming only the outermost tentacles of
nG GEA Ministry.
In the light of the underlying causes which led to the formation of the
.1 Ministry, it will be readily understood that in order to meet the changed
ituation in Greater East Asia, the Ministry was set up with the intention of
aking control of all metters, civil, naministrative, economic and cultural,
veluding only military affairs. Thus a wide sphere of matters which prior to
ne war had been handled by the Foreign Office were absorbed into the functions
f the GEa Ministry. For example, such important sections of the Foreign
fice as the Comnercial Affairs Bureau and the rolitical affairs Bureau were
bolished, and their functions were transferred en bloc to the GEA Ministry.
the basis for this move was that, after the China Incident had given way to the
reat Fast Asia har, there was no longer any "diplomecy" on the Continent.
il diplomatic negotiations with China were conduoted exclusively by the
ilitary, using direct military methods. Apart from these no reguler "peace-
ime" diplomatic negotiations were necessary. Thus the work of the Foreign
fice vas transferred to the GLA Ministry. And, the consulates-general and the
tbordinate diplomatic establishments, which bacame subordi-ate agencies of the
hanghai Embassy Office, naturally ceased to be diplomatic offices.
The following is a diagram of the consulates-general in Central China:

The Japanese Embassy in Nanking - the only Japanese diplomatic agency in
China.
As is clear from the above, the Shanghei Embassy Office and its suhordinate
onsular offices were diplomatic agencies in name only. m the matter of control,
hey were merely subordinate organizations of the GEA vzhistry. The only
iplomatic agency, properly speaking, was the Nanking mbassy, which had the
unction of denling with the Puppet Nanking Government.

nsulates were composed of career men from the Foreign Office, who contint
 think and act as such. They continued to act as "diplomate" and did not

Tgligence and impertinence were common. They were in constant disagreement
with the Japanese troops stetioned in their districts. As a result of preeaut

To cover up their resentment toward the military and their negligence, they
used the excuse that they wers 'orfieial: of the Foreign Office and not of the
G! Ministry". Furthermore, like Jayanese offic' ldom in general, these men
lacked the progressiveness and moral prineiples demanded by the times. They
s tuck to the principle of 'getting ahead" and their one thought and object was
to obtain promotion as soon as their time in grace had passed.
Levort Number II - A Summary of the Organization of the Central China Liaison
Bureau of the Asia Fromotion Board.
Since the Shanghai Embassy Office under the Greater East Asia Ministry
was nothing more than an extension of the Central China Liaison Bureau under
the sia Fromotion Board, in order to facilitate understanding of the aotivi-
.iss  the Embassy Office, a sketch of the orgonization of the Central China
lai. on Bureau follows:
1. Location of Burcau Offices and Names of First Bureau Chiefs.
The Asia Fromotion Board was established in March 1939 as an agency
irectly attached to the Cabinet. Liaison bureaus were set up in three places
and additional offices were established at three other locations. The
Locations of the bureaus and nemes of the first bureau chiefs were as follows:
1. Liaison Bureaus -
Nurth China Liaison Bureau (reiping)
Chief - Lt Gen MOnlOKA
Central China Liaison Bureau (Shanghai)
Chief - Vice Admiral TSUDA
Vice Chief - Maj Gen KUSUMOTO
South China Liaison Bureau (Amo:)
Chief - Vice Admiral CTA
2. Branch Offices -
Tsingtao Branch Office -
Tsingtao, Shantung Province
Kalgan Branch Office -
Kalgan, Inner Mongolia
Kwangtung Branch Office -
Canton, Kwangtung Frovince
Special Characteristics in the Structure of the Central China Liaison Bureau
Tnis organization was created for the purpose of unifying and coordinating
he execution of Japan's penetration policy in China after the China Incident.
 was the responsible office of the Asia Fromotion Board in Central China.
its special characteristics can be described as follows:
1. It was directly attachea to the Cabinet.
2.
Army and Navy personnel were appointed as civil officials without
giving up their active military status.
3.
It was a place of concentration for officials sent out from the various
home ministries in Japan.
(1) There were certain reasons for making this organization a direct
agency of the Cabinet. As a special agency for handling the Ching
CONFIDENTIAL
CT/173

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
Incident, it was independent of the various ministries and
ferea from no interference, facilitating the smooth exeouti
of policy, Another reason was that the Army and Navy men,
had long reacted against the "weakness" of Foreign Offioe or
ficiels, wished to evoid being associated with the Foreign or
fice and preferred an agancy on a higher level, directly attadk
to the Cabinet, which could freely accomplish their aims. The
conflict between the Army and Navy and the Foreign Ministry has
existed for any years. Moreover, such conflict was not necea
sarily limited - the Fer ign Ministry. It has gone on between
the civil cFiclals of various ministries and the military for
twenty years. Especially since the Manchurian Incident, the
military have heavily oporessed civil cfficials both at home and
abroad. Foreign Office officials in China were despised by the
anti-An lo-American Army men as "pro-Anglo-American dogs".
Thus, the setting up of the Liaison Bureaus under the
Tokyo Headquarters as agencies cirectly belonging to the Cabinet
was due to the situation then existing in Ja an as a result of
militarist politics.
(2)
In view of the above, the heart of this organization was in-
evitably composed of military men. Moreover, it was a special
feature that these men were not of the weaker groups, such as
the lst and 2nd heservists, but in the Active Service group.
Originelly, the Japanese civil service system was based on
the rule that "Militery men shall ot participate in politics",
and there were virtually no instances in which an active service
military man tecame e government official. It is true that the
Minister of War is an Army man on the active liet, and military
attaches of embassies or legations overseas cre also working
among civil officials, but there was no case in which Army and
Navy men on active service formed the core of any government
office.
However, after the Asia Promotion Board was established to
carry out Japan's China policy, both the Tokyo Headquarters and
the district liaison bureaus presented a strange picture with
Army and Navy men occupying the vital positions. Officers of
the rank of Army Colonel or Navy Captain, sometimes lieutenant
colonel or commander, were appointed "inspectors", and they
occupied the important posts in the district lisison bureaus.
Also, representatives of the various ministries in Japan,
such as the Ministries of Commerce and Industry, Finance,
Welfare, Civil Affairs, Home Affairs, Railways, etc., were
specially sent out to take various positions in the organization.
Here, too, the characteristic of a conglomeration of officials
from the various departments was arparent. Those appointed,
however, were by no means capable; on the contrary incapable
officials were selected. They represented only the sectionalism
of their respective ministries. lthough sent to Chine to
serve in the liaison bureaus, they had a very self-important
viewpoint. Neither did they study China nor did they conscien-
tiously try to carry out the national policy. Their sole object
was to make personal profit and to obtain promotion upon re-
turning to Japan after serving a term of 1-1/2 or 2 years.
These charecteristics were not limited to these special
government officials. Generally speaking, personal advangement
was the prime object of all government officials at home.

and Navy men as the core, specially delegated civil officiala
from various ministries, plus non-official experts in the
sconomic, social, cultural and various other fields,attaohed a
special staff. The organization was a consolidation of these
three groups.

The Political iffairs Department also included some junior civil
officials and non-reguler attaches of the War and Navy
Ministries. Further, high civil officials specially delegated
from various ministries were added to strengthen the department
and to providc the backbone of the Centrel Liaison Bureau.
Needless to say, however, tne direction of the Bureau was in
the hands of the Army and Navy inspectors.
Three official terms of service were completed, but by that
time the Army and Navy inspectors had come more and more to
realize that the military, by their own strergth, could not
achieve & final solution of the China Incident, and they also
realized that the difficulty of achieving such a solution through
the military was now keenly felt by the Japanese people in
general. As a result, compared to the first and secona terms of
office, there was a marked decline in the strength of the in-
spectors, and when the third term of office was reached, they
were losing their authority.
The Palitical Affairs Department was divided genorally into the
following sections:
(a) Political Affairs Section - As already pointed out, Army
and Navy men dominated this section and formed the nucleus
of the whole liaison bureau.
(b)
General Affairs Section - This section was made up of Army
and Navy officers of the rank of lieutenant colonel or com-
mander, holding office as inspectors.
(c)
Investigation Section -
Section Chief - Representative of Justice Ministry

The cnuses of Japan's defeat, in the government offices

overreached themselves, and in the fact that the fundamental
basis for such activities was poorly laid in the ideological
sphere. It is said that the arbitrary policy of the militarists
reduced the Chinese people to a state of desolation, but this
was due to the fact that there was no directing thought behind
it to fix the policy and determine its scope.
The Cultural Department should have devoted itself to the
working out of such directive ideas and thus laid the foundetion
for the settlement of the China Incident. However, it lacked
the capacity to do so.

D "sonnel Compcsition of Japa ese Enbassy Office in Shanghai.
The Shanghai Office of the Japnnese Embassy was establishec on 1 Nov 1942
as the local directive office of the Gel Ministry. The fact that its personnel
consisted of nothing more than a conglomeration of officials specially dis-
patched from various ministries in Japan has alrcady been outlined in a
separate report. In other vwords, it did not possess officials of its own, and
like the GEA Ministry, it was a combination of officials from various min-
iries centered around a core of representatives of the Ministries of War,
Nevy and Foreign ffairs.
The reasons for this organizational set-up may be outlined as followe:
(a) Being an extension of its antecedent, the Asia Promotion Board, the
Embassy Office retained the former's characteristic of being a com-
posite group of officials.
(0)
Sinse Japanese governmental machinery is feudalistic in character,
sectionalism is strongly entrenched. Tn order to avoid that tendency,
a "balance of power" arrangement was deemed necessary.
(2)
The various ministries pooled their strength in an attempt to curb
military oppression.
In shcrt, the root cause lies in the fact that the Japanese bureau-
cratic system is anti-democratic in nature.

3. Reasons for the Decline of the Power of the Military.
I heve repeatedly pointed out that Japanese governmental affairs have been
directed ty the military. In China military dictatorship was particularly
sovere, and the suffering which it imposed not only upon those in government
posts, but the local Japanese residents and the Chinese cannot be measured.
However, the tyrannical power of the military, under the stress of the difficul:
ar situation, notably declined, and the military were obliged to retreat.
The reasons for this mey be enumerated as follows:
(1)
Settlement of the China Incident grnau lly buceme more difficult,
and the repeated attempts of the Japanose Army to negotiate a general
peace vith the Chungking Government had become hopeless.
(2)
With the sharp change in the situation in the Greater East Asie War,
the military lost confidence.
(3)
The political, =conomic and iaeological policies of the military on
the spot were almost completely unsuccessful, and the resistance of
the Chinese people gave rise to an alarming situation.
der these circumstances, the Japanese military gradually lost confidence.
 c nt which must be particularly noted here is the outbreak of guerrila war-
are with Communist troops in Japanese occupicd territory. Because of this,
ace and order could not be maintained, and in turn the execution of economic
olicy, espccially as regards the buying-up of goods, became difficult. Plans
or the purchase and collection of commodities required by the Army, such as
odder, rice, wheat, oil, firewood and coal, did not go well. In the case of
aw cotton, which was among the most needed materials, the Army and Navy to-
gother, despite many obstacles pushed aheed their plans to purchase from the
northern Soochow area (north of the Yangtse River). They were hindered, howeve-
by the Cormunist Armies, and the final miserable result was that not one-half
of the necessary amounts were obtained. The failure in buying up materials
was partially due to the clumsy Japanese methods of trading and to the Chinese
people's non-selling policy. It was principally cuc, however, to the counter-
attacks of the Communist Armies, which made it impossible to preserve peace
and order.
Thus, the Army's stupid policies brought the administration of occupied
territory into serious trouble, and resistance by both the Communist Armies
and the people produced a state of helplessness and virtual collapse. Even
officials of the puppet Chinese government, though compliant on the surface,
did not necessarily act as directed by the Japanese military authorities.
Jader this iying process, the political and economic leadership of the Japanese
rmy weakened each year. The military, even though they knew that the aotual

Part II - Confliet Between ths Foreigm Office Group and Offisials from other

1. The Weakness of the Representatives of the Various Ministries.
As was explained briefly in Part I of this report, when the military had
lost confidence due to the failure of this China policy, it was the Foreign
Office group which outwardly grasped the right o leadership. In the Shenghai
Embassy Office, as the Foreign Office group gained ascendancy, the influence
of the representatives of the other ministries declined to a point even lower
than it had been when the military were at the peak of their power. The
reason for this was simple. When the military clique was in the saddle, both
the Foreign Office group and the group of officials from the other ministries
found themselves in the same boat, and they joined forces to present a united
ront. However, no sooner hed their powerful enemy, the military, lost in-
luence then the latent differences of power among the groups of civil of-
ficials came to the fore. The Foreign Office group showed that they had the
igor to displace the army men, and they began to look down upon the other
fi Is. Especially from about the time of the naval battle off Bouganville
n M.rch 1943, when the Greeter East Asia War had reached a critical stage,
ne Foreign Office group succeeded in aining control of the Shanghai Embassy
fice. Now the officials from the other ministries were naturally dominated
o. those from the Foreign Office and all matters of importance in all spheres
ere handled by the latter. A concrete illustration was the distribution of
posts. First, all officials in charge of general affairs were appointed from
e Foreign Office personnel. Especially, all informational work directed
owa d other countries was placed in the hands of Foreign Office chancellors,
dovn to the pàsitions of HANNIN (junior) rank. Not a single official of any
other ministry was allowed in this sphere. The representatives of the other
inistries were assigned to the following duties:
1.
Investigation
2.

4.

5.
Civil Affairs
6.

In short, political matters were reserved for Foreign Office officials
and all other officials had to accept assignment to other duties. Only
representatives of the Ministries of Commerce and Industry and Finance, oving
to the specialized nature of their work, were placed in charge of economic
natters within the Shanghai area and matters connected with finance and cur-
rerey. These officials combatted Foreign Office instrusion in their spheres.
"or one thing, the Forsign Office men were inexperienced in economics, finance,
:urrency, commerce and industry, and did not belong to the category of
pecialized experts. However, as they gradually supplanted the Army, the
Luntr was made of trying to put Foreign Office men in charge of economic,

.Dispute over the New Chine Policy.
The inside picture of the Shanghai Embassy Office was thus one of a
ieveloping struggle between the Foreign Office group, which was its main
irecting force, and the officials of the other ministries grouped together.
Looking at this conflict from the standpoint of policy, we see that it resolved
tself into a controversy over the "New Policy Towards China". I shall first
ouch upon what is meant by the "New Policy Towards China".

On 7 January 1943, Japan sought to give new expression to her impert
ormulas by fixing anew her basic policy for a settlement with China. 
urface reasons for this were:
(1) The relinquishment of extraterritorial rights
(2) The return of Foreign Concussions to China
(3) Recognition of China's autonomy and independence, etc.
In Central China, the new policy first manifested itself in the general
trunsfer of the political functions hela until then by the military to ad-
ministrative governmental offices. Further, all future political, economic
and cultural policies towards Chira were to be carried out on a footing of
equality from the standpoint of a common strategy against the United Stutes of.
Anerica. In other words, Japan's basic policy towards China underwent a
change of orientation. The necessity of such a revision arose from the retreat
of the military, as already mentioned, but in essence it stemmed from realiza-
tion of the stern fact that past policy based on mere lip-service to ethical
principles had been completely without effect, and that this must somehow be
remedied ty the working out of a new policy. fter the Greater East sia, War
broke out, it became apparent that Japan absolutely could not carry on any
kind of self-confident policy in China on the old basis. She, therefore,
attempted to escape from this crisis by means of a "heorientation of Policy".
Establishment of the Shanghai Embassy Office by he Greater East Asia Ministry,
following the dissolution of the Asia Promotion Board, was also a last resort
measure taken for the purpose of effecting the "New Policy Towards Chine".
Thus, at last, the stage was set for the application of the new policy.
(It is said that this policy was originally proposed by the Emperor. By this
means, it was calculcted that the Army and Navy could be easily induced to
poify their attitude of non-submission and Ja an could be quickly saved from
crisis). Within the Shanghai Embassy Office the new policy became the center
f fresh controversy between the officisls cf the various ministries. Apart
ron the disputes already mentioned concerning the allocation of duties, a
nf lon of opposing opinions arose regarding the new policy. The nature of
hes. differences was as follows:
Cn the one hand, the Foreign Office faction abstractly favored recognition
f the independent existence of the Nanking National Government and contended
het Ja an should deal with this gcvernment aiong the line of the diplomatic
sthods firmly adhered to by the Foreign Office in the past.
On the other hand, the group of officials of the other ministries contende:
that the National Government must not necessarily be thought of in abstract
torms, that in reality it wes nothing more than a sham government engineered
oy Japan, and that therefore the "New Policy Towards China" should be carried
out on the basis of recognition of this fact.
In other words, t he Foreign Ofrice faction, lacking knowledge and ex-
periunce in real politics and economics, attempted to carry out the new policy
towards China on an abstract basis, using the old diplomatic technique.
In part, this Foreign Offict attitude stemmd from a reaction against the
military ndministration in tne occupied zones. The militery had enforced its
orcrete political,economic and cultural policies rigidly, acting through
special duty branches set up in the various districts. Towards the Nanking
overnment, it concealed its actions behind other names but carried out a
policy of aggression and imperialism.
The Foreign Office group took the stand it was unethical to regard lightly
the Nanking Government which Japan had recognized. However, this does not mean
that their attitude was entirely logical. Behind their abstract arguments lay
pposition to the concrete policies of the military, Also they brought forward
he  ue of "abstract vs. concrete" simply as a tool to combet the viewa of
Leir rivals within the ofrice, the representatives of the other ministries.

4. The Struggle of the Foreign Office Group.
The Foreign Office group fell into the error of thinking that by virtue
of i+ grasp on the personnel make-up of the Shanghai Embassy Office, it could
mak the Office its own private preserve. However, under the existing
situation, especially the chaotic state of Sino-Japanese political and
-conomic relations centering around Shenghai, this outward organizational pre-
dominance was of no avail. In the execution of policy the Foreign Office
group constantly met with failure because it could not match the realistic
planning and ef ective mode of execution of the officials of the other
ministries.
In brief, the political and ecoromic problems of Central China as a whole
were too deep and broad for the Foreign Office group to cope with. After a
time the latter came to realize their own deficiencies in this respect, and the
then began to work upon the officials of the Finance Ministry with a view to
arawing them into their camp. By so doing, they hoped to curb the represent-
atives of the Ministry of Commerce and Industry, who remained numerically
strong. This secret design was partially successrul snd enabled the Foreign
Office to get its views on policy acceptea to some degree.
However, even though the Finance Ministry vas supposed to handle matters
of linance and currency, these matters in Centrel China were actually governed
by ihe Aray, and without the Army's permission, the Finance Ministry found
:toelf in the impossible position of being unable, in the end, to do anything.
∴lso, finance and currency were bound up with plans for the movement of goods,
ind without an understanding regarding the latter, the prc blem of financial
gulation was extremely difficult. Plans for the movement of goods were, in
goreral, controlled by the officials of the Ministry of Conmerce and Industry.
Thus, under actual circumstances, ti: authority of the Foreign Office was sub-
ject to certain fixed limitations. The Foreign Office group found itself
nable to give free rein to its ambitions, and despite its organizational
:upe ority, it had to yield to the strength of the Ministries of Finance and
cf  mmerce and Inaustry in the actual fields of their activity.
5. The Functicus of Counsellor OK:ZAKI.
Formerly, a director of the China Idustrial Development Bank (KikO KOGYO

Bank oi Japan in Japan. He was appointed to the Shanghai Embassy by the first

policy toward:China.
As his past career shows, OkizakI was a banker and in a sense a Finance
Ministry man. He had frequently lived in Shanghai and had wide connections
umong the Chinese. He also had the reputation of ossessing a profound knowl-
edge of economic problems. It was because of these factors that he was re-
cruited from the ranks of private individuals to take the post of counsellor
in March, 1943.
As a rivate individual ana a third party not connected with any faction,
CKiZ^kI's function was to mediate in the internal rivalry going on in the
Shanghai Iuhassy Office and to cast oil on the trouble waters. The Tokyo
Government, and Greater East Asia Minister OkI in particular, were deeply

the unreasonable attitude of the officials.
Hovever, despite his efforts, OK:Z KI could not overcome the narrow
minded mentality of the officials of the Foreign Office and the other ministrier
within the Embassy, and he was unable to bring about a smooth and rational
working environment. Moreover, coming up against the bureaucracy and feuda-
listic separatism which characterized the Embessy Office, his own thinking.
and personelity gradually became bureaucratic.
This is not a problem of OKiZAKI alone, nor of the Shanghai Embassy
Cifice alone. In a broad sense, it is a problem of the Japanese system and
the Japanese people as a whole.
The errors and irrationality stemming fpon the medieval character of the
lape se body politic have manifested themselves to the full in every sphere.
Tnus, the drama of internal struggle which went on in the Shanghai Embassy
(ffice must be recognized as nothing more than a local reflection of Japan
s & whole.

14 November, 1945,
SHANGHAI.
GREGORIO ABELARDO
(354 SEYMOUR ROAD, HOUSE 5, NO.12)
The following signed statement was made 6 November,
1945, by the SUBJECT:
"In January, 1942, a couple of weeks after the
capture of MANILA by the Jap., MR. CONRADO UY called
a Metting at the Metropole Garden which was attended
by the majority of local Filipino at which I was also
present. At the meeting MR. UY the purpose of the
FILIPINO ASSOCIATION was to work in the interest of
the Filipinos. But after all eforts have been made
to push the relief aim, the result was failure be-
cause the Jap failed to cooperate with the associa-
tions in giving required permits.
In Summer of 1943 DR. G. KALAMBAKAL sent letter
to the Filipino to attend metting at the (LIDO BALI
RooM) failure to attend it would mean arrest. The
real purpose of the metting was to discuss in regard
the arrest of four Filipinos but the matter was drop,
and instead we had election to revive the FILIPINO
ASSOCIATION.
On Oct. 14, 1943, we celebrate the Independence
day at the RACE COURSE. I was asked to pray and
thank GoD for having our independence and also prayed
for peace that all nations will be kind again to and
prayed the new government will lead the people in order
again.
At the celebration MR. CELIS was the Master of
Ceremonies and told the people the real meaning of the
Filipino flag. And MR. CASINO delivered speech thank-
ing Jap for giving us independence without asking and
reviewed the Filipino struggle for independence against
Spain and U.S.A. He also attacked America for not
giving us a chance to develop our own commerce trade
and industry.
SECRET

=== Page 29 === (confidence 0.99)
SEGRET
GREGORIO ABELARDO
At the CATHAY HOTEL the same day MR. CONTRERAS
acted as Master of Ceremonies in his speech he thank
Jap for being so kind in giving us our independence.
MR. ORDEN followed me CONTRERAS explaining the meaning
of our flag and then the last speaker was Mr. (uy).
MR. UY attacked America in his speech blaming the
Americans for the disunity of the Asiatic people and
also blamed the propaganda machine for misrepresenting
facts about Asiatic.
Signed: GREGORIO ABELARDO
Nov. 6, 1945."
EVALUATION: C-3.
DR. H. EVANGELISTA, pro-American Filipino leader,
who suffered arrest in the hands of the Japanese, made the
following signed statement 14 November, 1945:
"Re: G. ABELARDO: A pro-Jap and claims to be
religious; believes in mixing religion with politics.
He was believed to have stated that the Japs have ful-
filled what the Bible says, that colored people will
rule the world.
He is a strong believer and supporter
of KALAMBAKAL, CONTRERAS, BALDEVARONA and TRIA, the
most active pro-Jap leaders here in Shanghai; Member
of the Jap sponsorred FILIPINO ASSOCIATION; attended
meetings and gatherings of pro-Jap Filipinos; signed
petitions and resolutions addressed to Gen. TOJo and
to LAUREL'S GOVERNMENT; participated at the Indepen-
dence Parade, Ceremony and Reception on Oct. 14, 1943;
said the blessing and benediction at the Independence
Ceremony at the RACE COURSE on Oct. 14, 1943; signified
allegiance (Yes-man) to LAUREL'S GOVERNMENT. ABELARDO'S
associates were mostly pro-Japs and Yes-man and only
about two or three persons of the members of his band
were not pro-Japs.
Signed: H. EVANGELISTA."

13 November, 1945,
SHANGHAI.
FLORENCIO ALVAREZ
470/7-15 SEYMOUR ROAD.
1.
The following signed statement was submitted 31 Oct.,
1945, by the subject:
"31 October, 1945.
I hereby declare the following statements:
I am a musician by profession. I worked in the
NEW ZEALAND BALLROOM, then playing tea dances at the
MAJESTIC BALLROOM. Soon I got a chance in CAROL BALL
ROOM, then to the NEW PARADISE BALLROOM.
It was the time I was playing in the CAROL BALL
ROOM that the relief from the SWISS CONSULATE began.
Almost all of my fellow musicians began to get the said
relief. They told me to go and get a loan, but I
refused to go, having in mind that, I have a work and
can still maintain my living and can make both ends meet.
In my opinion the relief is for the ones who needed help
and for those without means to support their living.
The record at the Swiss Consulate will tell that I did
not approach for relief.
On or about 5 January, 1943, an association was
formed called the LIPUNANG PILIPINO.
There was a
meeting and MR. CONRADO UY was voted President. I
did not attend the meeting nor been a member of the
said association.
It was then DR. KALAMBAKAL steps in. There were
so many arrests among the Filipinos. As examples:
MESSRS. ANDRES ROSSETE, CANLAS and others by the
Japanese Gendarmerie.
DR. KALABAKAL called a meeting to all Filipinos
in Shanghai. Everybody must be present or face the
consequences. The meeting was held at the LIDO
BALLRoOM. The former officials resigned. DR. KALAM-
BAKAL was elected president. Almost all the Filipinos
SECRET

=== Page 32 === (confidence 0.99)
Page 2.
F. ALVAREZ (Cont'd): S E C R E T
were present. I was there too and signed my name
in the papers for those present at the meeting. We
were all scared not to attend the meeting because of
a threat of being arrested by the Japanese. I did
not vote in the election, first stayed and watched the
proceedings.
On 14 October, 1944, the Independence was given by
the Japanese.To me, Independence means something
to my heart and soul. A country free from foreign rule
as the Japanese made us believe was worth thinking about.
But things did not turn up as every Filipino expected.
The Japanese rule was worse after the Independence, where-
as the Americans during their period, gave the Filipinos
so many liberties as freedom of speech, religion and
others.
Coming to the point as the registration at the
Japanese Consulate, I pledgedallegiance to the New
Philippine Government, still thinking of the Independence
as noble and great, but it was just the opposite in the
long run.
A request was made by the Japanese to MR. CONTRERAS
to play music for their sick and wounded in the army and
navy hospitals. The augmented bands of MEE KOO MAY,
NEW PARADISE and LIDO was selec ted. All the boys from
the three places played for them.
During all these years, I have been working as a
musician and have strictly followed my profession.
All the above statements are correct and true to
the best of my knowledgeand before God I swear what I
have written.
Signed: FLORENCIO G. ALVAREZ."

Shanghai, 5 November, 1945.
CLARO ASUNCION
1.
The following signed statements was submitted by the
subject on 5 November, 1945:
"I hereby submit in details my activities during the
period from December 8, 1941, up to the day the peace
was declared.
On Sunday morning early in January, 1942, I attended
a meeting of Filipinos which was held at the METROPOLE
Ballroom in Gordon Road. On arriving there, I saw Mr.
UY already addressing the gathering explaining the pur-
pose of the meeting which was to organize an association.
Afterwards, Mr. UY called for nomination of candidates
for presidency, vice-presidency, secretary, treasurer
and publicity officer. Many candidates were nominated
and I was one among them. The provisional officers
elected were as follows, namely - President, CONRADO UY;
Vice-President, CLARO ASUNCION; Secretary, SIMEON DASMA-
RINAS; Treasurer, JUAN TRIA; and Publicity Officer,
VICENTE CASINO.  The name of the association was approved
to be THE FILIPINO ASSOCIATION of SHANGHAI. After a
lapse of about two months, I received a telephone call
from Mr. UY informing me that the association had already
acquired a temporary office at No.66 Nanking Road, and
asked me to call at the office after my office hours.
At that same afternoon, we the officers of the association,
discussed plans for a Mianight Ball. Since then, I began
to attend the office preparing in conjunction with my co-
officers all the necessary steps to be done for the success
of the proposed Midnight Ball. In spite of all our efforts
to do something for the benefit of our needy countrymen,
this Midnight Ball failed, because we were not allowed by
the Gendarmerie to hold it. When Mr. UY made his tour of
lecture to Nanking and other places, he left the office
under my care. During the tenure of my office which was
about one month, there was nothing of importance that had
happened except the issuing of identification cards to
Filipinos, mostly musicians. When Mr. UY came back and
took charge of the office again, all the provisional
officers of the association agreed to pass a resolution
whereby a manifesto be circulated among the Filipino commu-

As one of the members of the Filipino delegation, I feel very much
honored to be present here to express to you all, our profoundest joy
for being invited to perticipate in the celebretion of this auspicious
event, China Youth Day. When I say this, I sincerely believe that I
am not only expressing the sentiment of the delegation itself, but
al so that of the Filipino Community in Shanghai, which I have the
honour to represent.
The significance of this memorable event, is to promote mutual
friendship and cooperetion among youth orgenisations of the nations
in the Greater East Asia Jo-Prosperity Sphere, namely, China, Japan,
Manchoukuo, Philippines, India, Burma, Thailand and others. In my
short broadcast, I wish to tell you regarding my impressions on this
happy event. The camp wiere all the various units of the delegations
are encamped, is very spacious and ideally located. The tents are
arranged in such a way that resemble a real military camp end give no
abstruction to the free movement of the youths. The rules and
regulations in the camp are strictly observed, and a brotherly atmos-
phere is enjoyed by all the participants. The parade to the Mausoleum
of Dr. SUN YAT E and to the tombs of the Chinese and Japanese martyrs
of war, which was participated by the entire internetional delegations,
was very traditional and impressive. The picnic which was held after
the parade where different kinds of performances were held by every
unit of the delegation, was very significant and enjoyable. All of
these successes, I should say were entirely due to the well organised
administrative committee of the celebration. Finally, I wish to
mention here that the united feelings of the youths who participated
in this celebration, is e clear proof towards the successful unifi-
cation of the Asiatics.
Before conclu ding, allow me to express once more, our deepest
gratitude for the generous hospitality afforded to us. I have said.
Si med: OLARO ASUNOION."

I, G. B. MENDOZA, do hereby make the following

On or about June, 1943, I began to suspect the

There Were C. UY, M. LAMSON, C. ASUNCION, V. CASINO.
They used to hold their meetings behind closed doors
in UY'S office at THE SHANGHAI TIMES. They met at
least three times a month. Putting two and two
together, I am fully convinced that it had something
to do with Filipino affairs during the war.
Aside from the meetings above mentioned, these
fellows used to meet in ALOBOG'S store with some
Japanese where they used to discuss Filipino affairs.
I found this out from our telephone operator who used
to overhear their conversation on the telephone."

playing draft (Dama). He said he wanted to see SAMPER,

he wanted to talk with me. I asked him whether in private
or with my friends, but he replied, "Alone". So JOQUICO
and TEODORO went out. While we were alone, he opened his
papers in typewriting and I saw so many names. He ques-
tioned me something like this. "Do you know EVANGELISTA?"
"Yes sir.""How long?"
"Many years." "Is EVANGELISTA
a captain in the S.V.C.?" "Yes sir, he was my commander
before." "Is he an officer in the Philippines?" "I
don't know sir, he never told me anything." "Does he get
power from the SWISS CONSULATE?" "I don't know sir. I
have requested EVANGELISTA many times but he could do nothing."
Then he wrote something in his note book in Japanese, con-
tents of which I don't know. I was quite frightened that
I forgot to ask him where he came from and his name.

"The Japanese who arrested me asked me why I did not
recognize the new government. I said, "I have pledged
allegiance to the United States government and I determine

asked me repeatedly whether the Swiss Consul or Dr. EVAN-

government. I said, "None of them did; my conviction is
entirely my own."
My arrest and imprisonment was brought about through
the whole hearted collaboration of the pro-Japanese group
headed by J. CONTRERAS, V. ALOBOG, J. ALINDADA, C. UY,
G. KALAMBAKAL, V. CASIÑO, R. JOQUÍCO, F. SAMPER and J.
PAJARILLO, alias "CLARO ASUNCION." They were constantly

said. They eagerly supplied the Japanese informations."

15 November, 1945,
SHANGHAI.
TEODORICO BACLAY
The SUBJECT, a Filipino musician, in late December,
1941 and the first three and a half months of 1942, was
active in promoting Filipino cooperation with the Japanese.
He was seen in the company of Japanese officials and on
15 Apri1, 1942 left for MANILA on a trip sponsored by the
Japanese authorities. Filipino informants in Shanghai
believe the purpose of this trip was espionage. At present
the SUBJECT is believed to be in the PHILIPPINES, though
there are rumours that he was slain by guerillas.
The following is an article (with picture) which
appeared in the 30 May, 1942 issue of the CLARION FILIPINO,
a Pro-Japanese Filipino paper published in SHANGHAI:
"FIRST S'HAI FILIPINO ON VISIT TO P.I.
With a view to testifying to the great work planned
and accomplished by Japan toward the establishment of a
New Order in Greater East Asia, MR. TEODORICO BACLAY,
Shanghai resident for many years and active member of
the FILIPINO ASSOCIATION of SHANGHAI left here for MANILA
on April 15. Special permission for the trip was granted
by Japanese authorities here.
MR. BACLAY was the first Filipino to return to the
islands since the outbreak of the War of Greater East Asia
on December 8.
The 32-year old Filipino resident plans to make a tour
of his native province of CEBU as soon as possible, planning
there to contact his former schoolmates and urge that they
extend their wholehearted cooperation in the reconstruction
work already launched by the new Central Government of the
Philippines."

November 16, 1945.
DEMETRIO BALDEVARONA (VALDEVARONA).
The subject, a former Filipino Shanghai musician,
fell under the influence of pro-Japanese elements after
the outbreak of the war; he used his influence with the
Filipino community to enforce co-operation and collabora
tion with the enemy.
In May, 1944, he left Shanghai abpard a Japanese
military plane for the Philippines, presumably to act as
spy and informer for the Japanese.  In a signed statement
Paul Aquino, Shanghai Filipino, told of being approached
by CONkADO UY, to go to the Philippines for the purpose
of ascertaining which prominent Filipinos were anti-
Japanese.
It is believed that the subject received
a similar proposition, and unlike Aquino, accepted it.
At present the subject is believed to be in Manila,
although there are rumours that he has met his death.

68
Re. D. Baldevarona.

Shanghai, 16th October,1945.
" I, P.C. ESPINO, make this statement of my own
free will and without duress of any kind.
At the outbreak of the war I was associated with
Dr. H. Evangelista at the American Association.
Our object was to unite the Filipinos to back up the
Allied cause.
We also tried to counteract Japanese
moves to get the Filipinos on their side.
When the Filipinos called at the American Associa-
tion to register we spoke to them one by one on this
subject.
D. Valdevarona came to the door of the Amer.Assoc.
and attempted to stop the registration of Filipinos and
went to Mr. Anderson, president, and asked what authority
the American Ass ociation had to register Filipinos since
Filipinos did not come under American jurisdiction any-
more and it was dangerous for them to register since Japan
was at war with America. He asked where the money was
to come from for the Filipino relief.
He also attempted to dissuade myself and Evangelista
from registering Filipinos and said: " We didn't start
this war. It is not our war. The Japanese may stop this
SEORET

=== Page 57 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
registration and relief at any moment and
then the Filipinos will suffer. Japan is stronger
and richer than Americans and they cm do whatever
they want. If the Filipinos and Americans get
relief it is only because the Japanese allow this.
It wi ll soon be stopped."
I heard that the Japanese were watching me around
February 1943 and I was arrested 14 March 1943 by
three Japanese gendarmes (including Hirano who was
in charge). I was taken to Bridge House after they
searched my house and seized all documents and
pictures. I was not allowed to talk or say
goodbye to my family.
I was questioned by Hirano and the others at home
and at Bridge House. They asked: "Do yau know
Evangelista, J. Canlas, A. Roseta, S. Toledo (now
deceased)? What did you do last Christmas? (they
were suspicious of my part in raising funds for
Xmas dinner for war prisoners)."
At Bridge House they examined my papers but found
nothing incriminating. I was then put in confine-
ment for the night.
The second day I was fed two meals but not questioned.
The third day I was questioned again (Hirano) and
asked: " Why did you refuse to join the (Japanese
sponsored) Filipino Association?"
On the paper he held while he questioned me I
noticed Dr. Kalanbakal's signature. He showed me
this paper.
He then asked me why I solicited Filipinos to
get funds for the dinner. He showed me this paper
also and it was signed by Valdevarona.
(signed P.C. ESPINO)

DEMETRIO BALDEVARONA (VALDEVARONA).

news about the Japanese and Filipinos in Shanghai and

Americans will come back.

Evangelista.
I kept quiet. Then A. Navarro told me
that if I would not say what they wanted me to say to
the Jap I could not come out of jail. The Jap told me
he must investigate me again. A. Navarro and his gang
left telling me not to forget what they told me to say
sp that I could be released.........."

The following statement was made on 22nd October
1945, by F. SALVADOR, Pr o-Japanese Filipino.
0
I, F. Salvador, make this statement of my own free will.
In March 1943 Kalanbakal inviteà me to tea also present
were Alfonso Navarro, Demetrio Valdevarona and T. Cruz.
After tea we got in a car and I was surprised when we
arrived at the American School and were taken into a room
with Japanese in uniform. Kalanbakal spoke in Japanese
to these men and I did not understand what he said.
Kalanbakal spoke to us in Tagalog and instructed us
to tell the Japanese about Dr. Evangelista and other pro-
American Filipinos. The Japanese showed me a picture of
Dr. E. and I said I knew him. Theynasked me if he was a
spy and I did not answer yes or no. I can't remember what
the others answered.
The Japanese held a small piece of paper in his hand
as he asked us abot the Filipinos and whether we knew of any
Filipinos who are pro-American and making propaganda against
the Japanese.
Dr. Kalanbakal then took this piece of paper and said:

I think Navarro went back the next day but I don't

"MAXIMINO CADA, ardent supporter of the Japs, had
shown his feelings in all discussions of war situation.

military tactics. He told us in the argument of his
strong belief in final triumph of the Japs and the Ger-

were fighting, that is Asia for the Asiatics. Whenever he
hears of ill-treatment of the Jap to Filipinos, he stand
up and in a loud voice blame the Filipinos for not co-
operating with the Japs. CADA was well versed with all
local Japanese and Filipino activities unknown to most
Filipinos. When BALDEVARONA was sent to P.I. by the Japs,
which I believe on espionage purposes, CADA made the
following comment, "Why did not the Japanese send me to
the P.I. instead of BALDEVARONA?" He waged a bet of 100
to 1 that Japan will win the war. This affair was heard
by all the members of the band in CIRO'S BALLROOM. (Autumn
of 1943.)"

10 November, 1945,
SHANGHAI.
MARCELINO CADELINA
(Lane 1025, House 46. BUBBLING WELL ROAD.)
1.
The following signed statement was made by the subject
on 4 November, 1945:
"Shanghai, 4 November, 1945.
TO WHOM IT MAY CONCERN
"I came to Shanghai 2 Dec, 1945 from TIENTSIN. For
three months after I had no job except for an occasional
extra here and there. About the month of February, a
chance came to go to SooCHOw. We could go only to
SOOCHOW through a special pass which KALAMBAKAL said he
could take through the Japanese office off DIXWELL Road.
The Japanese officer told us the first step is to join
the FILIPINO ASSOCIATION. Much as I disliked it I had
to join it as I was in sore need of a job. As it turned
out to be the pass was delayed and the place had to open
soon, so they sent two of us with three other Chinese
musicians to open saying (KALAMBAKAL) that the pass would
follow soon.Our boss had to get, through bribery, fake
passes for us as Chinese, as the passes didn't materialize.
Because of this episode I became cautious.
I came back to Shanghai after three months.
I attended the LIDO meeting and nominated MR. A.Y. CELIS
for President against KALAMBAKAL, who threatened my country-
men and whom I began to hate. Next day I was surprised to
learn I was one of the committees as I didn't hear of any
election for committee. I wanted to decline the office
but I was afraid someone might start to investigate me and
my past and come to the Soochow episode, so I had to attend
two committee meetings and one general meeting. The first
committee meeting was held at Mr. J. ALINDADA'S place and
talk about some benefit dances and boxing matches to help
some members of the FILIPINO COMMUNITY. The second was
held at Mr. CELIS' and talk about the last meeting, after-
wards a press photographer came and took a group picture.
The general meeting was held at the LIDO BALLROOM as a
SECRET

=== Page 71 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
MARCELINO CADELINA (COntinUed):
farewell to KALAMBAKAL. After this I lost connection
with the Association and I don't know what happened to
it.
From January of 1943 I had been an active member of
the F.M.U. First as committee from January to March 1943,
then Treasurer to March, 1945, and Secretary to Juns.
I have been in close contact with Mr. CELIS and I
have not heard him utter a pro or anti-Japanese remark.
He told me that he was offered a position by the Japanese,
but he declined it.
During the registration at the CATHEDRAL I went there
with the avowed intention of answering "no" thinking that
the question would be: Do you pledge allegiance to the
Japanese? But when I found out that the question was:
Do you pledge allegiance to the Republic of the Philip-
pines? it seemed to me a sacrilege to answer no, just like
denying my own Country, so I came the next day and answered
"yes". In my heart there is no pro-Japanese nor anti-
American sentiment.
I do hereby declare that I signed a petition or the
telegram to TOJO asking for independence and also the
Constitution of the LIPUNANG PILIPINO.
To His Excellency
General HIDEKI TOJO
Prime Minister
TOKYO, Japan.
WHEREAS We take this opportunity of paying our
sincerest respects to the Imperial Japanese
Government, their leaders and soldiers;
WHEREAS
We admire your overwhelming triumph in
Malaya, Burma, East Indies and the Philippines;
WHEREAS
We appreciate that through the untiring
efforts of your dauntless army, navy and air
force, the Anglo-American imperialism has
vanished from the Orient where peace once more
reigns;
WHEREAS
We receive with joy the glad tidings pro-
nounced by your Excellency in the Diet pro-
mising us our independence;

under threat to join the FILIPINO ASSOCIATION and to play at the
Midnight Ball scheduled to take place at the PARADISE BALLROCM on
4pril 25th, 1942. In the eVening of April 17th Mr. JOSE C. CONTRERAS
asked me to explain why I was forming another organization against
the FILIPINO ASSOCIATION which was already formed for the whole
Filipino Community under the protection of the Japanese. Mr. CoNTRE.
RAs accused me of persuading the musicians not to play at the proposed
Midnight Ball which affair was cancelled owing to a telephone call
received by the CENTRAL POLICE STATION advising them that trouble
would accur if the ball was held. Consequently the affair was
cancelled. The FILIPINO MUSICIANS UNION was formally inaugurated on
Lay 1st, 1942, and in the evening of May 12th I was assaulted by
Messrs.'ALFONZO NAVARRO and CONTRERAS and dismissed from work by Mr.
CONTRERAS immediately. F. M. U. members increased inspite of
Mr. CONTRERAS' instructions to all musicians not to join this pro-
American organization, the F. M. U.
- II -
DR. KALAMBAKAL'S BACK LIST
In the presence of the musicians in March, 1943 at CIR0'S
BALLROOM. I asked Dr. KALAMBAKAL whether it was true that he wanted
me to be arrested and he answered that my name was included in his
Black List. So I went to the SWISS CONSULATE to enquire whether
any step could be taken by the CONSULATE in connection with those
arrest during the month, as I thought I was then on my way to the
same fate. Mr. ESSIG replied that as this was a political case all
they could do was to report the matter to the U.S. Government.

important subject to be discussed will be the present situation of

- IV -
ACCUSATIONS AGAINST ME
On May 31st, 1943, Dr. KALAMBAKAL, together with his pro-
Japanese clique, Messrs. CONTRERAS, ALOBOG, ALINDADA and CONSTAN-
TINO reported to the authorities to have me arrested. I was
summoned at the CENTRAL POLICE STATION where I found myself
before said clique. With the inspector-in-charge of the crime
branch presiding, the aforementioned honourables fires the
following accusations against me:-
1. Mr. CONTRERAS accused me of heading a pro-Americem
Organization. He begged the presiding Jap to dissolve
the F.M.U. or bring our Union under the control of the
FILIPINO ASSOCIATION on the following grounds:-
(a) The F.M.U. was financially supported by the Americans
through the SWISS CONSULATE.
(b) Between the lines of the Rules of the F.M.U. could
be inferred American propaganda.
(c) The F.M.U. was opposed to the pro-Japanese aims of
the FILIPINO ASSOCIATION (LIPUNANG PÍLIPINO)
(a) Mr. CELIS' failure to instruct the F.M.U. members
to join the FILIPINO ASSOCIATION.
2. Mr. CONTRERAS stated that I was receiving a fabulous
amount as relief loan from the SWISS CONSULATE.
3. Dr. KALAMBAKAL alleged that I branded the bandleaders
present "CROOKS".
4. Mr. BENNY CONSTANTINO seconded Dr. KALAMBAKAL's accusa-
tion.

- V -
INAUGURATION OF PHILIPPINE INDEPENDENCE
On October 10th, 1943, Mr. CONTRERAS, as Acting President
of the FILIFINO ASSOCIATION, took charge personally of everything
pertaining to the parade and ceremony at the RACE COURSE in the
morning, while Mr. CONRADO A. UY and V.Z. CASINO, together with
V.D. ALOBOG as Chairman of the Reception Committee. made all
necessary arrangements including financial matters, at the
CATHAY HOTEL in the afternoon. The Minutes of the Meeting
written by LORENZO EUGENIO on MEE KOO MAY letterhead and the
Memorandum in the handwriting of Mr. CONTRERAS, written in green
ink testify to all of his efforts to make the day a complete
success. Mr. ORDEN and I raised the FILIPINO flag and I erplained
its symbol. Mr. ORDEN spoke on the same teneur at the CATHAY
HOTEL in the afternoon.
Messrs. UY and CASINO in their fiery speeches attached the
mericans, as may be read in the SHANGHAI TIMES dated October
15, 1943.
Correction of a newspaper item is attached hereto.
- VI -
DEPARTURE OF DR. KALAMBAKAL
To bury the deceased body of the FILIPINO ASSOCIATION, its
Board of Directors lead by the writer, resigned en block on
announcement that DR. KALAMBAKAL is leaving SHANGHAI. This
doctor was pleased to receive our resignation for he saw a loyal-
ist in the person of Mir. JOSE C. CONTRERAS, an heir to his lofty
aims and pro-Japanese ideals.

- VIII -
SECOND SUMMON BY THE POLICE ON MONDAY
Februery 28th 1944.
On Friday evening, February 25th, 1944, a Jap detective ordered
me to report at the CENTRAL POLICE STATION on the following Monday.
On arrival at the station on the latter date, the Japanese Inspector
in charge cross-examined me on complaints about my activities. The
charges against me were exactly the same as the accusations fired by
MeSSTS. KALAMBAKAL, CONTRERAS, ALOBOG, ALINDADA and CONSTANTINO
on May 3lst, 1943, The Japanese Police officer in his statement
recognising me as a good community leader advised me to cooperate and
be in good terms with Messrs. UY, and CONTRERAS. and join their
Filipino Community Board. I promised to do so provided that the
self-appointed committee would be accepted by the Filipino Comunity
and work for the welfare of our poeple. On my return home at noon
I was surprised to learn of the arrest of some Filipinos.
- IX -
APPOINTMENT AND RESIGNATION AS VICE-CHAIRMAN
OF RELIEF COMMITTEE
On written notification from Mr. CAsINO I went to see the
Japanese Vice Consul, Mr. HAYASIDA, who advised me that in accordance
with the meeting of Consular staff, it was agreed that Mr. UY, CON-
TRERAS and myself were appointed Chairman and Vice Chairman respec-
tively of a Relief Committee to assist the Filipinos. Mr. HAYASIDA
informed me that he had already interviewed Messrs. UY and CONTRERAS
and they had signified their willingness to serve in the committee.
Mr. HAYASIDA made me understand specifically that the relief loan
from the SWISS CONSULATE would be discontinued. After about half an
hour's hesitation, I promised to do my best. On reaching home,I
arafted and forwarded a letter of resignation, copy of which is
attached hereto. Messrs. UY and CONTRERAS still persuaded me to
withdraw my resignation but on being adamant, Mr. BENNY CONSTANTINO
took my post as Vice-Chairman. In accordance with circular letter
of the Japanese Consulate No.22 dated November 22, 1944, I was asked
twice by Mr. CONSTANTINO to report at the Japanese Consulate, but
to date I have NOT done so.

CANDIDACY OF MR. CONTRERAS AS
F.M.U. PRESIDENT
During the election in March, 1944, Mr. CONTRERAS in
presenting his candidacy as President, promised bigger midnight
balls whose permits he could easily secure from the Japanese
authorities or relief from the government of the PHILIPPINE
REPUBLIC. He failed miserably in this election because the
voters were well aware of his empty promises. In March 1945
F.M.U. election he succeeded because of his promise to increase
the sslary of musicians and give up POLITICS. (What does he
mean by this word?).

I was arrested on February 28, 1944 together with Dr.

enese. (3) Being the tool of Dr. EVANGELISTA and Mr. ESPINO for

Government (6) For being a member of an association working
against Japan of which Dr. EVANGELISTA is the leader.
I was tortured very much because I refused to admit the above
associations. I suffered mental torture because I was made to sit
on the floor facing the wal' for 17 hours a day, for 63 days. We
were transferred to the High Military Court at Kiangwan for 2l days
after which we received our sentence. Dr. EVANGELISTA got 3 years
and the rest of us 2 years each. On Apil 29, 1944. we were hand-
cuffed and we were sent to Ward Rd. Jail where I was known as S-35.
We were released together on Mey 30th, 1945, on the second mass re-
lease of "J" prisoners. The Japanese that tortured me are the

Evaluation C-3
5. JUAN TRIA, pro - Japanese Filipino, submitted the following
statement on 22 0ctober 1945 :
One evening, while I was returning home from my business, I
met on the street Mr. SUGANO, in charge of the foreign section, he
asked me to go to his office anytime whenever I am free. I said
yes. After few deys passed I went to see him, but while I was
going to the Police Headquarters, I saw coming out Mr. AGAPITo CELIs.
he was in state of hurry, he did not even notice me, so I proceeded
on and found Mr. SUGANO. When I sat down near his table I saw I
list headed by Dr. EVANGELISTA and Mr. ESPINO then FREDDY MATEO.
PRIMITIVO CRUZ Mr. JAVIER and some others more. I could not
remember all the names, because did not let me read all. After few
minutes, he asked me if I know those people, I said yes, and the
asked me some other questions as follows:
Did you meet anybody outside when you was coming in? I
said, yes, Chinese Policemen.
Again, asked me, whether I did not see any Filipino? I
again said, No.
Then I already suspected that was something wrong, but I pretended
that I did not see anybody.
Again, he asked me, if I know much about these Filipinos in
the list, their political views and activities? I said, again I
dont know. because I have no time for anybody specially for poli-
tical affairs, because I am very busy with my business and work.
because at night I work as kusician and the day as a broker.
because I am trying to make as much money I could to meet ail the
expenses. I said last, that I know them only as Filipinos, that's
all. Knowing that he could get nothing from me, he told me that I
could go. That's all.
(signed) JUAN TRIA.

To His Excellency
General Hideki Tojo
Prime Minister
TOKYO, Japan
WHEREAS
We take this opportunity of paying our sincerest
respects to the Imperial Japanese Government, their
leaders and soldiers;
WHEREAS
We admire your overwhelming triumph in Malaya, Burma.
East Indies and the Philippines;
WHEREAS
We appreciate that through the untiring efforts of your
dauntless army, navy and air force. the Anglo-merican
imperialism has vanished from the Orient where peace
once more reigns;
WHEREAS
We receive with joy the glad tidings pronounced by your
Excellency in the Diet promising us our independence;
RESOLVED
That we, officers of the LIPUNAN PILIPINO, on behalf of
the Filipino Residents of Shanghai, go on record to con-
vey to the Imperial Japanese Government, through your
Excellency, the assurance of our full co-operation in
the establiahment of the GREATER EAST ASIA CO-PROSPERITY
SPHERE.

BIDASTO (VEDASTO) CONDE
The following signed statement was made on 19 November 1945
by DR. EVANGELISTA. pro-American Filipino leader :
"CONDE is a musician by profession. During the war, e
dealed in selling mostly contraband or military controlled cargoes
together with some Japanese and was said to have accumulated plenty
of money.
He is a pro-Jap and was seen and moved only in a Japanese
atmosphere. He was used by CONTRERAS, pro-Jap leader, as an
interpreter and is believed to be a paid informer by the Japs.
CONDE. never attended the Filipino Association meetings, never
registered as a Filipino at the Jap Gendarm office; did not regis-
ter at the American Association; never registeredat the Shanghai
American and Swiss Consulate and did not register at the CATHEDRAL
where every Filipino was required by the Jap Consulate to register.
I believe he was under protection of the Jap Military aathorities
or at least has an understanding with them or would have been
punished for disobeying Jap orders.
Once, CONDE came to the SWISS CONSULATE and tried to apply
for relief; he gave as an estimate for his daily living expenses
every item in term of Yen or Jap money, and as he was never
registered at the SWISS CONSULATE, his request was rejected.
From information I gathered from others, CoNDE, was never
in need of relief and was very well off financially.
His brother in TIENTSIN was one of the most active pro-Jap
Filipino leader there."
(signed) H.C. EVANGELISTA

30 Cotober19
JCSE CONTRERAS (175 House 12 Seymour Road)
The Subject on 20 October 1945 submitted statements
reproduced herein as well as certain notes, minutes, consti-
tution, petitions, resolutions and other matters pertaining
to the Filipino Association. These documents which Contreras
was careful not to sign despite the signatures of otherFili-
pinos, together with photographs furnished by him, were a
valuable asset to the investigation of the Filipino community.
The Subject admitted during visits to this office that he was
in touch with the Japanese in regard to organizing the Filipinos
and that me wore a Japanese armband when assisting in the regis-
tration of Filipinos who signed "no" or "yes" in swearing alle-
giance to the puppet government of Jose Laurel. Subject also
admitted playing at benefit shows for the Japanese.
Contrera's statements follow:
20 October 1945
"I, Jose Contreras, make this statement voluntarily and
without compulsion.
I have heard with my own ears Conrado Uy and Vincente
Casino make anti-American speeches in public.
Some time before Cct. 14, 1943 I visited Uy in his office
to discuss plans for the Independence celebrations.
Soon after the outbreak of the war I was approached by a
group of Chinese ex-gangsters who said that they wereifrm the
Nanking government. They asked me and my band to play for Charity
and I complied and appeared at the Grand Theater. If the benefits
of this were used to buy airplanes I was not aware of it. Even if
this was true I would have complied since everyone was frightened
at this time.
In late 1942 I was approached by one Yamada, supposed to be
an attache to the Jap Gendarmerie. He said he planned to hold a
concert to make some side money, it was called the Greater East
Asia concert and took place at the ueen's Theater.
At this time F.Mateos had his name crossed out by Yamada at
his own request as he did not want to play in the concert, at the
¿ueen's Theater. Yamada was later exiled from China for blackmail.
I knew Takeda from Jessfield Road Gendarmerie, Terakado and
Hori from the My burg Gendarmerie, also Motomura interpreter at
this latter station.
I voted "yes" at the Japanese consulate and gave allegiance
to the Laurel government because 1) I thought that Secretary Vargas
was purposely left by Quezon in the Philippines to take care of
problems 2) since I was never in sympathy with the Japanese which
Is proven by my not signing the constitution of the Filipino Asso-
ciation I would rather answer "yes" so that they would not with-
draw what little confidence they had in me; I thought that as
SECRET

=== Page 124 === (confidence 1.00)
SECRET
JOSE CONTRERAS (Continued).
leader of the Filipino community it would be better if I
was able to maintain this little confidence so the Japs
would listen to me once in a while. I knew I could prove
I believed in allied victory.
I did not go to the Swiss Consulate forrelief.
I played at Japanese barracks twice at the command of
Dr. Kalambakal.
I was requested by one Iwatate, of Domei news, to take
part in a radio program with my band on the first anniversary
of the independence of the Philippines. He asked meto make
a speech but I declined about two days before the broadcast.
During the broadcast he thrust a piece of paper (several)
pages) in my hand and when I asked what it was he said:"Just
read it for me." It was very hard to talk and he then announced
something and beckoned to me. I read this statement which was
in English over the air but since I was excited over pronoun-
ciation and my stammer that I cannot even now remember what it
said. It seems to me at this time that it was something about
the GEA sphere. I remember it said one thing in particular,
though. That was "we don't hate the Anglo-Americans." This was
circled in pencil. I can't remember what happened to this paper.
I made speeches thanking the Japanese for their magnimity
in granting independence to the Philippines at the time of
Independence Day celebrations but I said nothing against the
Americans.
The path I picked in regard to the war was a tough one
since I had the responsibilities of the community whose welfare
I took to my heart, perhaps I stumbled once or twice on my way
to Calvary in the hands of the Japanese but I was not nailed to
the cross because they were not aware of the fact that I did not
sign the constitution which gave collaboration with Japan. In
trying to do what I thought was best I might have made a few mis-
takes.
I was elected by a committee of nine to be vice chairman of
a committee of board control. This was to be connecting link
between Lipunang Pilipinos which seemed to be very inactive and
the Filipino Musicians' Union which could do little, it being
non-political. hen the registration was announced the Japanese
called the control board to maintain discipline and teach the
Filipinos to fill in the blanks. I did do this and helped fill
in blanks. I did not give advice to anyone on how to fill the
blank in."
(Signed): JOSE C. CONTRERAS.

JOSE CONTRERAS (Continued).
Shanghai, Oct. 22, 1945.
"Several weeks after the registration of the Filipinos
at the Japanese Consulate, Vice Consul Iisuka asked me what
should be done with the "no" men; whether they should be put
in a reformatory institution wherein the Filipinos should be
give hard labor and Orientalization. Those becoming Orienta-
lized soonest to be released soonest. I advised him against
this.
Before registration I approached Dr.Evangelista and
asked him to come to my dancehall and be my guest and I added
that if he would do so I would do anything he said as far as
advice goes. If the doctor id done this I am sure that I
would have been able to lead the Filipino community against
the Japs. The doctor did not come and that is why I had to
sign "yes"."
(Signed): JOSE C. CONTRERAS
