4:24 A.M.
October 20, 1945
Shanghai, China
MEMORANDUM
"Mr.Pinder,
U.S.A.Liaison Cffice
53 Foochow Rd.
Sir:
Do you remember one of braham Lincoln's famous sayings,
"Tell me who your friends are and I'll tell you who you are?"
But if I should tell you that the Filipinos in Shanghai are all
my friends, at least potentially, except"two bad boys", I don't
think it will help you much in your investigation regarding my
activities during the war. Allow me then to philosophy on life's
eternity: I am what my parents were, and my children shall be
what I am. Of course I must give allowances to the effects of
invironment on a human being at his age of puberty and of ado-
lescence.
To begin with, I was bprn in the icol Regions, the south-
eastern peninsula of Luzon, known to me to have never participa-
ted in the war in 1898 between the ünited States of America and
the Philippines. On December 16, 1916 (during the first World War)
my younger brother was born,baptized LIBERATO WOODRON VILSON CONTRE
RAS Y CANET. (LIBERATO meanS LIBERTY). LIBERATO was suggested
by my father who was inspired by your statue of Liberty. WooDROW
VILSON was my mother's suggestion, for to my dear mother, WOODROW
WILSON, then your President, was the man of the hour.
SEORET

=== Page 126 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
JOSE CONTRERAS (Continued).
In 1917-1918, while the United States of America was
at war for the sake of Democracy, I received my first educa-
tion. A little later, my oldest brother Francisco, inspired
by the Filipino hero in that first World War, Tomas Claudie,
surprised my father when he informed him that he had enlisted
in the Philippine Melitia. "Surprised" because Francisco was
only sixteen years of age!
At my age of puberty, I learned a good lesson from my
father. I was in the fourth grade. Cne day the class was on
strike. The classroom teacher was going to be changed. I was
the brightest in the class. I thought the change won't matter.
Ly progress in school depended on me. The whole class walked
out except me. Nhen I arrived home I proudly told my father
that the whole class made a "big trouble" and walked out
except me, since I was a "good boy". The next thing I knew
I was down on the floor. My father gave me too strong a slap
for my tender age. fter a while I managed to stand up. My father,
his eyes piercing mine with an almost hypnotic effect on my
trembling body, said in a commanding voice:"Peping (that's my
nickname), remember this as long as you live,-'What the majority
does y-oo-uu got to follow, you rat'". I did never forget, Mr.
Pinder, even during the recent war, when the welfare of my
community concerned me most. But I also learned long before that
if the majority cannot move because of pressing circumstances,
then I got to LEAD. Hence I accepted the Acting-Presidency of
the Lipunang Pilipino.
Now here I am suspected a pro-Japanese, naturally because
I was the Acting-President of the Lipunang Pilipino, the associa-
tion supposed to be in sympathy with Japan. How am I to prove
that my pro- mericanism which dates back from y childhood days,
inspired me to pioneer your Swing Music in China in 1937; made
me dream to go to the United States of America these last six
years, and "swing it out there", - how am I to prove that my
pro-mericanism was not lost in the chaos in Shanghai, especially
during the first stages of the Pacific War when Japan was supposed
to be winning???
Precisely I thought of that. That proofs arebetter than
documentary evidences, especially when no one could be trusted
during the war. Even Dr.Evangelista who I suppose is pro-American
made me doubt him when he gave a party to the Japanese at the Sun
Sun Restaurant. So I took hold of the Association's file in order
to save my neck from the Japs, for if they knew a fact concerning
a document of the Association, I would have been in a jam. Had I
been jailed by the Japs because of that document, I would not have
minded it but for the fact that I did not trust Dr.Evangelista and
Espino, the former being decidedly anti-Filipino and the latter
having manifested some traits of the "modern adventurer".
SECRET

=== Page 127 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
JOSE CONTRERAS (Continued).
I am still wondering why Dr.Evangelista resigned from
the S.V.C. just when the Pacific War was almost at sight, while
others remained. And can you imagine that he told one Filipino
to jump out of the window of the Swiss Consulate just because
he requested him to grant his loan one day earlier since this
Filipino musician was going out of Shanghai next day to assume
a job? I also heard that he told one Filipino lady who ms asking
for coal allowance to eat the meat and vegetables raw in order
to save coal expenses, and that if she should get sick she could
take injections? If only you could gather us together in a meet-
ing you would save much time in your investigations and you'll
understand why the Filipinos were divided into two factions, the
"yes" group and the "no" group when we are essentially one as I
had proven since I was elected President of the Filipino Musicians
Union on March 12th last (the members of which were mostly "no"
men) till the end of the war. I wonder if I'm wrong when I say
that Dr.Evangelista together with ambitious Espino failed in
preserving the oneness of the Filipino community in Shanghai, or
should I say, since the Filipinos are essentially one in favor
of the Americans, that they failed to let us answer all "no"
during the registmation at the Holy Trinity Church on November
18 and 19, 1943.
During my Acting-Presidency, I had rendered the Lipunang
Pilipino, a dangerous weapon, virtually useless, since that had
been my aim even before I became Acting-President.
Lost of the Filipinos here as you probably know now are
musicians. On September 1, 1943, the Cabarets were ordered to
close at 10:00 p.m. instead of the usual closing hour of 12:00
midnight, with the accompanying rumour that they would be closed
very soon either due to coal shortage of the Power Company or
that since in Japan all cabarets had already been closed down
by the government Shanghai cabarets would follow, or both; or
that Shanghai would be bombeu very soon and that the people were
expected to be home early in order to be able to protect their
families, and that should bombs begin to fall, naturally the
cabarets would be closed like in 1937 when the Chinese and Japa-
nese were fighting in Shanghai.
Therefore in November, 1943, just before the registration,
the Filipinos figured to depend more on loans than on their jobs.
To the common Filipino, their duty was to support their families,
nothingmore. Their minds reached only up to their stomachs and
I don't blame them. The war was not their business, at least most
of them did not know what America and Japan were fighting for.
So the Filipinos receiving substantiallloans answered "no" while
those whose loans were a joke and those without loans could not
answer "no" since they might antagonize the Japs who might in turn
instruct the cabaret owners to kick them out of the jobs, and they
might be jobless and"loanless".
SECRET

Page:6
JOSE CONTRERAS (Continued).
Regarding the adventurous Espino, I would like to kmow
why he tried to stop Mr.Miranda's petition for loan after the
war. And why he told one Filipino who in turn mads the rest of
us know that those who would not join the Reception Band would
not receive any relief loans.
I remember that you told me I was prosperous during the
war. May I inform you that since 1940 I have been the highest
paid bandleader in Shanghai with my salary in one cabaret alone,
while this sum is trebled since I have three cabarets in my hands.
For reference, kindly look over our registration papers where
you will find that while the average salary of a musician in
November, 1943, was between C.R.B. Two Thousand to Three Thousand
Dollars, mine was Thirty Thousand Dollars. To be more detailed
you can look over the payroll of the cabarets where I work and
find out for yourself.
bay I also inform you that I was notified by the Japanese
in October 1944 that the loans for the "yes" mon had arrived but
I did not get it. In May this year I was called again and I turned
it down. (Dirty money).
Lastly I suggest that you forget about that Registration at
the Holy Trinity Church since that was never authentic."

JOSE CONTRERAS (Continued).
When this same Contreras was organizing concerts for the
Japanese he said in public: "If you boys refuse to entertain
the Japanese and refuse to go with me you will get into trouble.
I don't know what will happen to you."
In October, 1945, I was reading a newspaper story of Dr.
"vangeliota's experiences under the Japanese. Contreras spoke
to me and I asked him if the Americans had asked him any quest-
ions about it. He said: "Yes, they ask me questions but after
questions they will say that Contreras is very smart and clever.
I can bully them. I knew how to get in and I know how to get out.
I am like Laurel, I know what to do to keep out of trouble."
In September, 1945, B.Constantino stated to me:
"I'm not worried about the mericans I've only been cooperat-
ing with the Japanese for a few months while Contreras has been
cooperating: for three years. If Contreras goes to jail for three
years I'll only be in jail a few months for this."
About a year ago Contreras said to me: "Why don't you
Filipinos cooperate us officials, after all, even if the Americans
come back we are only small fry and they will not punish us."
(Signed): PABLO MOLINA.

DEPARTURE OF DR.KALAMBAKAL.
" To bury the diseased body of the Filipino Association,
its Board of Directors lead by the writer, resigned on block
on announcement that Dr.Kalambakal is leaving Shanghai. This
doctor was pleased to received our resignation for he saw a

REGISTRATICN AT THE CATHEDRAL SCHOOL IN NOV. 1943.
The same party, essrs.Uy, Contreras, Casino, Alobog,
Asuncion and Lamson, with their colorful green arm band, took
charge of the registration, persuading our people to pledge
allegiance to the Philippine Republic by answering YES.
SECOND SUI. CN BY THE POLICE CN MONDAY, FebruarY 28,1944.
On Friday evening, February 25th, 1944, a Jap detective
ordered me to report at the Central Police Station on the follow-
ing Monday. On arrival at the station on the latter date, the
Japanese Inspector in charge cross-examined me on complaints
about my activities. The charges against me were exactly the
same as the accusations fired by Messrs.Kalambakal, Contreras,
Alobog, lindada and Constantino on May 31st, 1943. The Japanese
Police officer in his statement recognising me as a good commu-
nity leader advised me to cooperate and be in good terms with
Lessrs.Uy, and Contreras, and join their Filipino Community Board.
I promised to do so provided that the self-appointed committee
would be accepted by the Filipino Community and work for the
welfare of our people. (n my return home at noon I was surprised
to learn of the arrest of some Filipinos.
APPOINTMENT AND RESIGNATION AS VICE-CHAIRLAN OF RELIEF COMMITTEE
n written notification from Mr.Casino I went to see the
Japanese Vice Consul, r.Hayasida, who advised me that in accor-
dance with the meeting of Consular staff, it was agreed that Mr.
Uy, Contreras and myself were appointed Chairman and Vice Chairman
respectively of a Relief Committee to assist the Filipinos. Mr.
Hayasida informed me that he had already interviewed Messrs.Uy and
Contreras and they had signified their willingness to serve in the
committee. Mr.Hayasida made me understand esficifically that the
relief loan from the Swiss Consulate would be discontinued. After
about half an hour's hesitation, I promised to do my best. On
reaching home, I drafted and forwarded a letter of resignation,
copy of which is attached hereto. Messrs.Uy and Contreras still
persuaded me to withdraw my resignation but on being adamant, Mr.
Benny Constantino took my post as Vice-Chairman. In accordance with
circular letter of the Japanese Consulate No.22 dated November 22,
1944, I was asked twice by Mr.Constantino to report at the Japanese
Consulate, but to date I have NOT done so.

TERSO CRUZ
The following is an extract taken from a signed statement
made 17 October, 1945, by JOSE CANLAS concerning his arrest
19 March, 1943:
"One week after my arrest, I was taken out of my
cell and was taken into a room and asked me the following
questions: "Do I love my Country, I said yes; Do I like
the Americans, I said yes, and then HIRANO punched me in
the face several times.
Then he read a list of names and asked if I knew them.
Included were KALANBAKAL (yes), ALFONSO NAVARRO (yes),
DEMTRIO VALDEVARONA (yeS) FRANCISCO SALVADOR (yeS) T. CRUZ
(yes). All of these except SALVADOR I had heard were
cooperating with the Japs.
HIRANO then read me an affidavit signed by these men
accusing me of being anti-Japanese, an American propagan-
dist and an American spy. Though, in substance, this
was true I denied it.  I was beaten and forced to kneel
with a piece of wood thrust behind my knees.
They asked me if I received instructions from DR.
EVANGELISTA and asked for information concerning him
and ESPINO. I replied that I did not know anything.
Twice they asked me if I would continue to go around
town and I replied yes, it was necessary to my business.
He asked me if I didn't get paid by DR. EVANGELISTA and
if DR. E. didn't get paid by the Americans. I said no
and was slapped. He then asked if DR. E. had told me to
tell the boys in the orchestra not to sign up on the side
of the Japs. I said no. Slapped again.
HIRANO then asked me if I wanted to cooperate with
the Japanese. I said that I would accept any job.
(However I did not cooperate and did not work for them).
I was then removed to a cell ......"
EVALUATION: C-3.

and swore allegiance to the puppet Government of the
Philippines.
On 14 October, 1945, he took part in a parade and
ceremonies celebrating the independence of the puppet
Philippines regime.

The following is an extract from a signed statement
made by A. ROSETE on 25 October, 1945, telling of his
arrest by the Japanese on 17 March, 1943:
"About a few days after, I was taken out of my
cell and brought to a room where I saw DR. KALAMBAKAL,
D. BALDEVARONA, A. NAVARRO, F. SALVADOR, T. CRUZ and
the Jap investigator, all sitting around a big round
table.
BALDEVARONA brought out Dr. EVANGELISTA'S picture in
military uniform and said. "Here is your American Chief's
picture." I answered, "He is our Chief." Then A.
NAVARRO told me that they came there because they wanted
to take me out of jail on condition that I must tell the
Jap that I have been telling DR. EVANGELISTA news about
Japanese and Filipinos in Shanghai and that I do not co-
operate with the Japanese because the Americans will come
back.
SECRET

=== Page 142 === (confidence 1.00)
Page
SECRET
TERSO CRUZ
I was wondering what their intention was for they
were forcing me to say the above statement. As the
above conversation was in Tagalog, the Jap told us
that we should talk in English. I was silent all the
while because I was thinking what to say. Then the
Jap toId me to say all what I know about DR. EVANGELISTA.
I kept quiet. Then A. NAVARRO told me that if I would
not say what they wanted me to say to the Japanese, I
could not come out of jail. The Jap told me that he
must investigate me again.A. NAVARRO and his gang left
telling me not to forget what they told me to say so
that I could be released. I did not answer him for I
knew that if I tell what he told me to say, since it was
not true, it might bring trouble to DR. EVANGELISTA and
myself also.
I was investigated again, but by another investigator
but the same interpreter. They cross-examined me and
they were kinder this time, but I did not trust them.
I told the investigator that I did not like what the five
Filipinos were teaching me to say because by that way I
am a spy. The investigator said that NAVARRO and his
gang told the Jap that I am not a good man. I answered
that they are the ones who are bad because they are
smokers of heroine. The investigator look at me and
shook his head. He talked to the interpreter in Japanese
I was returned to my cell."

16 November, 1945,
SHANGHAI.
I. DIZON
(360 AVENUE DU ROI ALBERT, HOUSE 1)
The following is a signed statement made by the SUBJECT
on 3 November, 1945:
"Dear Sir:
With regard to information concerning MR. CONRADO
UY, all what I know is that I heard him giving a speech
over the radio against the United States government.
I know that he used to go quite often to the Japanese
Consulate, but what they talked about I have no idea.
DR. KALAMBAKALL and MR. CONRADO UY formed a new
organization called the LIPUNAN FILIPINO. The first
time a meeting was called, only a few Filipinos attended.
Because of this, DR. KALAMBAKALL and MR. CONRADO UY
threatened to put the Filipinos in jail in the AMERICAN
SCHOOL. This news scattered around. A second meeting
was called and this time all the Filipinos turned up.
When I attended these meetings, it was known that
the society would be called the FILÍPINOS RESIDENTS
ASSOCIATION. A paper was given to us in which we had
to sign our names for membership. Most of us signed
our names, but we later found out that the association
was not the FILIPINO RESIDENTS ASSOCIATION but the
LIPUNAN FILIPINO, a Pro-Japanese Society.
The meeting was held at the LIDO BALLROOM.
DR. KALAMBAKALL was elected by the Filipinos as the
President of the LIPUNAN FILIPINO.
This election was
forced. We Filipinos, were threatened that the
Japanese would put us in jail, and besides DON JOSE
(JOSE ALINDADA) shouted to his musi c ians that "all the
LIDO boys turn to the right" so all were compelled to
SECRET

=== Page 146 === (confidence 0.99)
Page 2.
SECRET
I. DIZON
stand up. This was how finally, DR. KALAMBAKALL
was elected as President.
A few months after this, DR. KALAMBAKALL was
supposed to return to the Philippines. He turned
all his responsibilities to MR. JOSE CONTRERAS as
the acting chairman. Since MR. CONTRERAS was
chairman of this association, he never asked for a
meeting or anything concerning the association, for
reasons which I don't know.
I know that MR. CONTRERAS used to visit the
Japanese Consulate quite often for purposes which I
do not know. I have worked for him for about six
years, and I have found him to be a man who never
tells of his activities.
As I have no interest whatsoever in politics, this
is all what I can tell you.
Yours truly,
Signed:
I. DIZON."
Signed: A. C. PINDER (Signed)

Filipinos to cooperate with the Japanese.

R. DIZON
(LANE 281. ROUTE VALLON. HOUSE 4)
The following signed statements were made by the
SUBJECT on 3 November, 1945:
"I, RICARDO B. DIZON, do hereby make the following
statement of my own free will:
That J. ALINDADA,who was at that time my band-
leader, convinced his musicians to vote for KALAMBACAL
for the presidency of the FILIPINO ASSOCIATION for the
simple reason that KALAMBACAL was very influential to
the Japanese and whatever he asked the Japanese will
be granted.
ALINDADA is a staunch supporter of
KALAMBAKAL. I have heard him say at a certain time
while we were having our dinner (all his musicians to-
gether), "Why should we like the Americans since we
are orientals and they discriminate and insult us?"
That before the concert at the QUEEN'S theater (I
could not remember the date) two Japanese whom I believed
to be of high rank in the Japanese government service,
came to interview him at LIDO'S BALLROOM. After the
Japs left, he came to us and told us that the Japs re-
cognized only two Filipino big shots and he referred to
himself as one.
That JOQUICO and SaMpER used to voice their dis-
satisfaction with the SWISS CONSULATE regarding the
granting of loans to the Filipinos. SAMPER has especially
told me of his hatred of DR. EVANGELISTA and ESPINO.
That MADRASO, a very close friend of C. UY, has told
others that he knew beforehand that DR. EVANGELISTA would
be arrested by the Japanese. After the arrest of DR.
EVANGELISTA and his gang, he told methat the Japanese are
punishing them severely, inflicting all conceivable tor-
tures. He told this in a conversation with me at the
CANIDROME.
He is known to be a staunch supporter of the
Japanese.
SECRET

=== Page 149 === (confidence 0.99)
Page 2.
SEC RET
R. DIZON
UY, widely known to be the father of the FILIPINO
ASSOCIATION, cooperated with KALAMBACAL and threatened
all Filipinos of arrest by the Japanese if they refused
to be members of the said Association. I believe that
he was the head of the gang that had six pro-American
leaders arrested. He is known as an arch-anti-American
judging from his speeches and articles in the CLARIoN
and in the SHANGHAI TIMES. UY has tried his best to
make the Japs authorities to put the NO-MEN in camp. UY
induced the police not to issue resident certificates to
Filipinos unless they produce membership card with the
said association, but after a considerable argument be-
tween CANLAS and the police, the latter phoned the CENTRAL
POLICE STATION who finally consented to issue resident
certificates. UY has also said that he would have the
Japanese authorities stop all food rationing to Filipinos
unless they are members of the FILIPINO ASSOCIATION.
That CONTRERAS became the president of the FILIPINO
ASSOCIATION after KALAMBACAL left Shanghai. He presided
in all the meetings and delivered speeches also in the
presence of some Japanese. CONTRERAS called a meeting
in his house few days before the independence celebrations.
Among those present were SALES, E. LORENZO, and others.
That I used to have an argument with LEON MALABANAN
and in the argument he praised the Japanese and upheld
their ideals thus exposing his anti-Americanism. He
once made a coment and betted that some Filipinos would
be arrested by the Japanese.
That in my several conversations with M. CADA, he made
several statement of his pro-Japanism, praising the
superiority of the Japs in military tactics, their power,
their patriotism, and their discipline. In our dis-
cussion, he exposed his hatred to America and to DR. EVAN-
GELISTA. I made a comment that why should he hate
America if he hated DR. EVANGELISTA, since there is no
connection between DR. EVANGELISTA and America.
I took part in only two concerts because I was forced
by J. ALINDADA, Once at the JAPANESE NAVAL HOSPITAL and the
next was at the QUEEN'S THEATRE. We were paid for the
concert at the QUEEN'S THEATRE, but only very little. We
believed that J. ALINDADA pocketed a great portion of the
money. I know him to be very proficient in this.

3rd November, 1945.
ROMON V. JOQUICO. Lane 41. House 10 Carter Road.
1. The following signed statement was submitted
voluntarily by the subject on October 15, 1945.
n
How I came to be a Director of the
LIPUNANG FILIPINO. SHANGHAI.
About the beginning of April 1943, I received
a circular letter, dated April 8.1943, and signed
by Dr. Gertrudo Kalambakal.
The substance of this letter was that a
meeting would be held on April 18,1943, at 9.00 a.
at the Lido Ballroom on Medhurst Road. Every
Filipino in Shanghai was asked to attend this meet-
ing which would be for the purpose of discussing the
arrest of some of our compatriots and our precarious
situation. This letter ended with the threat that
dire consequences would follow failure to attend
said meeting. (Note. The arrested persons referred
to were Evangelista, Espino, Carlos and Andres, who
were taken into custody by the Japanese Gendarmerie
some time the previous month)
In view of the fact that these four persons
were still under detention when Kalambakal circularized
the Filipino community and since everybody believed
that he was behind their misfortune, when the meeting
was called to order on the morning of April 18th, the
Lido Ballroom was packed with Filipinos.
When I got to the Lido Ballroom I saw Espino and
Carlos. (Later I learned that together with Evangelista,
they were released the previous day, while Andres was
released earlier.) As if to give weight to Kalambakal's
threat, present also were several Japspsome in uniform
and some in plain clothes.
After the preliminary formalities, the meeting
got under way, with Mr. Conrado Uy in the chair. He
made a rather long speech in English, the substance of
which, as far as I can remember now, was that as president
of the Filipino Association he was ashemed to say that
SECRET

=== Page 162 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
RAMON V. JOQUICO (COntinued).
he had not accomplished anything to the benefit of the
local Filipino community and that the local Filipinos
on their part must also be ashamed fer their failure
to give him their full cooperation and support in his
endeavours to better their situation. He ended by
tendering en bloc his resignation and that of his col-
leagues as officers of the Filipino Association.
Kalambakal then took the chair. What happened
next, I can't quite remember. I think he got up
and started to make a speech in Tagalog about his experiences
in the Philippines, painting himself as having always
been a champion of the under-gog, and he related how he
was called "the angel of the Philippines." because at one
time, he said. he got the Japs to release some starving
Filipinos who were caught by the Japs stealing rice and
were bound with rope.
Mr. A.Y. Celis got impatient and internupted
Kalambakal's peroration.He obtained permission to
speak and he pointed out that according to Kalambakal's
circular letter of April Sth, the purpose of this meeting
was to discuss the arrest of the four Filipinos mentioned
above, and he asked Kalambakal to explain forthwith the
reason for their arrest.
I can't recall Kalambakal's
reply, but I beliey, he denied any connection with their
arrestand told Celis that if he wanted to know the reason
he should ask the Japanese Gendarmerie.
After that evasive reply. bedlam broke loese, many
fellows soth anti and pro Kalambakal clamored at the same
time for the right. to speak. Finally order was restored
and the meeting proceeded, but at this period my memory
seems to be a blank regardin what followed. I am not
sure whether at this juncture Mr. Jose Contreras obtained
the floor and harangued the udience to the effect that
being orientals the Filipinos should for et the past
and thenceforth assimilate oriental culture. He said that
although he limself was wearing occidental clothes, yet he
was an oriental in his sentiments. He also dared any one
among the Filipinos present +o stand up and declare him-
self pro-American. (He spoke in English)
S E C R E T

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
RAON V. JOQUICO (Continued).
I think that a discussion of the Filipino Association
then followed.
I believe some suggested to carry on with
Uy's organization while others advocated the formation of a
new one. Here my memory again fails me. I cannot recollect
which suggestion was adopted but I remember that some one
(I think it was Mr. A. Sales) suggested that the association
be called Lipunang Filipino instead of Filipino Association.
The motion was seconded and adopted.
The decision was then made to nomin te and elect
officers right then and there.
Kalambakal ceded the chair
to one of his men, Mr. D. Valdevarona, and stepped down from
the rostrum. He and Celis were nominated candidates for
president.
As the Lipunang Filipino as yet had no constitution,
the voting procedure came in for a lot of heated argument.
Celis demanded for secret balloting while Kalambakal asked
for counting of raised hands. Some way or another,Kalambakal's
wishes prevailed.
When voting for Kalamhakal took place, he must
have been greatly chagrined to see that only a few hands were
raised but Mr. Jose Alindada came to his rescue and claimed
that as it was difficult to count the raised hands, it would
be better if the voters stood up when the name of the candi-
date they favored was called to indicate that they were voting
for him. Celis agreed to this new procedure and when
Kalambakal's name was called, Alindada's now famous battle-
cry, "Lido boys, up!" rang throughout the big ballroom, and
the Lido boys (his band) and a few others stood up. But even
this failed to produce a majority of votes for Kalambakal,
as most of the audience (including mvself) remained seated.
Alindada must be a resourceful fellow and at his suggestion
the voting procedure was once more changed and again I heard
his voice. but this time it was, "Those for Dr. Kalambakal,
stand on the right, and those for Celis, on the left,"
followed by the terse command, "Lido boys, to the right!"
This new procedure caused a lot of confusion, because what
was Alindada's right was left to the audience, and as majority
of the voters started moving towards the left, Alindada be-
came frantic and started waving his two arms in opposite direc-
tions, in the meantime shouting directions, and the confusion
became greater. (Truly, it was a great farce.)
I seized this opportunity to edge my way out
of the ballroom by the back door and go home, because at that
S EC R ET

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
Page 4.
RAMON V. JOQUICO (Continued).
time I was suffering from rheumatism in both feet,
my ankles being badly swollen, and as it happened
to be a rainy day, the pain became almost unbearable.
I got into a rickshaw and went home. Shortly
after arriving at home, Mr. T.. Villaflor and Mr.F.L.
Samper came together. Samper and his famtly were
living with me at that time. Villaflor whom I saw at
the meeting came to see my feet at my request. (Later
he brought me some pills which helped to ease the
pain.)
Sometime later, Salvador Samper, F.L. Samper's
vounger brother, also came to my home. As soon as he
entered my room, Salvador told me that I had been
elected a director of the Lipunang Filipino and
that it was he who submitted my name at the meeting.
I upraided him for being a meddlesome fellow and told
him that he had no right to submit my name without
my consent. I told him I had no desire to be iden-
tified with the Lipunang Filipino and he said that
if that was the case he would ask Kalambakal to delete
my name from the list of directors.
I believe that it is quite relevant to mention
here that soon after Evanglista, Espino, Carlos
and Andres had been taken into custody by the Japanese
Gendarmerie, I learned from F.L. Samper that he was
asked by Kalambakal to go with him to the Gendarmerie
Headquarters on Avenue Petain to answer some questions.
R.L. Samper also told me that he had learned that y
name was on the Jap black-list, and that Kalambakal had
asked him for information about my movements and accord-
ing to him he told Kalambakal that as far as he knew
I stayed at home most of the time and that I neither
went to people's houses nor nosed into their affairs.
In view of this information and what happened to
the four Filipinos mentioned above, naturally I had to
watch my step in dealing with Kalambakal. Therefore,
when Salvador Samper said that he weuld ask him to
delete my name, I told Salvador to let matters stand
as they were.
I raked my brains for some reason
to resign without incurring Kalambakel's animosity,
but I c~uld not find one that I thoughtwould bear
close scrutiny by such a wily old fox as Kalambakal.

=== Page 165 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
Page 5.
RAMON V. JOQUICO(COntinued).
However, a solution to my problem presented itself
unexpectedly. Exactly two and a half months after I had
been elected, willy-nilly, a director, we had an officers
and directors! meeting. During this meeting a bitter
altercation took place between Kalambakal and Celis
(president and vice-president, respectively) and as a
result a resolution was made that, with the exception of
the secretary and treasurer, all the other officers as
well as directors of the Lipunang Filipino should resign
and a fresh election held. This resolution was eventually
put into effect and on July 10, 1943, I handed my resigna-
tion to the secretary.
In conclusion, I might add that during the short
period I was a director, Kalambakal and Celis did nothing
to foster soliaarity and cooperation, but rather dissension
among the local Filipinos.
Signed: B.V. Joquico
Shangha1, October 15,1945."

GERTRUDO KALAMBAKAL
There is evidence to show that the subject was in Shanghai in
1925 when he was engaged in writing radical and communistic leaflets
which he had printed in NAnkING and distributed to Filipinos.
Presumably he spent the years between 1925 and 1941 in Shanghai and
in Japan where he claimed he had attended Medical School. He
re-appeared in Shanghai in November 1941 end was associated with a.
Dr. TONGU who claimed to be a Japanese at FOO MING Hospital in
North Szechuen Road. In early December 194l, the subject, according
to evidence, told V. ALOBOG, 78 Nanking Road, who in turn told Dr.
EVANGELIST that he was leaving for the Philippines in order to help
the Japanese and that war would be declared in two days.
Ir. EVANGELISTA states that he left Shanghai in a Japanese Navy
boat in early December. Upon his return to Shanghai. KALAMBAKAL
claimed to have landed in the Philippines aboard a Japanese plane.
In late January or February 1942, he returned to Shanghai. His pi
ture appeared in the SHANGHAI TIMES on February 9, 1942, together
with Japanese newsmen whom he was telling about the situation in the
Philippines.
The subject claimed to have received an appointment with the
Japanese Navy in Shenghai as Advisor and showed certain papers to
confirm this. Certain Filipinos claim to have seen the subject in
Jepanese ilitary uniform. These informants claimed that they saw
Japanese soldiers saluting and bowing to the subject. The subject
claimed to be backed and financed by the Japanese. The subject
brought back with him from the Philippines, Japanese printed pesos.
During the period of February 1942 to April 1943, as wil1 be
shown oy signed statements, the subject worked to enlist Filipinos
in the Japanese cause. During this period he was instrumental in
causing the arrest of certain pro-American Filipinos. On April 18.
1943, he was elected President of the LIPUNANG PILIPINO. During
June and July, the subject wrote, signed and circulated for signa-
tures, petitions and resolutions adoressed to the Enemy pledging
Filipino co-operation and lauding them upop their achievements.
The subject resigned as President of the LIPUNANG PILIPINO on
10 July 1943 as he claimed he was making a trip to TOkYO and the
Philippines. No further information is available concerning his
whereabouts though some Filipino informants state that he is dead.
The subject was born in SANTA CRUZ, LAGUNA Province, Philippine
Islends and is 50-55 years old. He spoke Japanese fluentiy and is
easily mistaken for a Japanese, being 5'1 in height, weight 100-110
pòunds, Stature very slight, face; small, complexion: very dark,
skin: rough.
Evaluation C-3
Upon his return from the Philippines in February 1942. the
subject circulated leaflets in English and Tagalog. Though on these
appeared the date of 18 December 1941, they were believed to be
printed in Shenghai. The wording of the two leeflets we have in our
possession are similar, the English version being as follows :
SECRET

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
Pago2.
GERTRUDO KALAMBAKAL
"My dear countrymen:
You must think over our beloved country is now in the critical
situation. This does not belong to us. This belongs to Japan and
U.S.A. For this reason the Imperial Japanese troops came here to
fight against their enemy, not to the Filipino people. The Japa-
nese aim is to drive out the American power here in the Far East.
The Japanese policy at present is to help the whole Asiatic
peoples to be free from the power of his enemy. The Japanese troops
came to our islands without any intention to take any territory.
Now is the time we Asiatic peoples should unite and at the same
time have close relation each other and take swords to our common
cause. The Japanese, the Chinese, the Thei people, the Malayan
peoples and our Filipino people are brothers. We find several
different way in custom dress and religion, but in almost other ways
we are same. If this is true you must cooperate with the Japanese
soldiers and not better fight against them, because they are not our
enemy. If we resist our country will be cestroyed and the lives of
our people will be regretful.
My countrymen we must find out that the intention of Japan is
for our benefit, and the principal aim and policy of the Japanese
Government at present is to help all Asiatic nations for the emanci-
pation of their liberties. Do not believe the American people any
more. It is 4l years now since merica got her sovereignity in our
island but all her promises for our freedom were in vain. We must
study the present condition of her diplomacy.
I repeat to you that the Japanese troops who landed on our soil
are to fight against the merican soldiers, end at the same time
they help the Filipino people to take independence by force. not by
word. This is our opportunity to join the Japanese troops and fight
against the enemy of our liberty. after the peace is established
the Japanese Government will recognize and grant our independence.
If you are the true ilipino who love your mother country, I
hoe, you will make a good relation this time with the Japanese
soldiers to take freedom of our country. Your humble countrymen,
I talked to you in the same feeling as you wish to have independence
of the Philippines."
BAGONG LIWAYWAY.

Evaluation A1
On 15 April 1943, the following article appeared in the
SHANGHAI EVNING POST with the subject's picture :
"FILIPINOS HERE SET AIL TO GIVE MORE COOPERATION TO JAPAN IDEAL
In order to enable Filipinos in Shanghai to do their share
in supporting Japan's cause in the GREATER EAST ASIA war and
thereby attain the independence of the Philippines as promised
by Premier General HIDEKI TOJO in his speech on January 28, local
Filipinos will hold an open meetin& at the LIDO BALLROOM on
Sunday morning when the existing FILIPINO ASSOCIATION will be
reorganizeå end new officials elected, Dr. GERTRUDO KALAMBAKAL
well-known Filipino patriot, revealed to the SHANGHAI EVENING
POST in an exclusive interview today.
Dr. KALAMBAKAL, who, with the late Dr. SUN YAT-SEN and
other prominent Asietic leaders, founded the Pan-Asiatic Asso-
ciation in 1922 in Shanghai. said that he felt that the time
had come when Filipinos in Shanghai should play a more active
part toward the estaplishment of the GREATER EAST ASIA CO-PROS
PERITY SPHERE.
JAPAN'S GREAT TASK
"Japan", Dr. KAlABAKAL declared, has set out to achieve
the great task of emancipating all the Asiatic peoples on the
basis of racial equality, and thereby give the Asiatic nations
the freedom and independence which they have long desired.
"We Filipinos," he went on, "are very grateful to these
aims of the Japanese Government and realize that it is also up
to us to do our share by cooperating with Japen."
TO ELECT NEW OFFICERS
Dr. KALAMBAkAL said that at Sunday's meeting new officers
SECRET

=== Page 173 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
GERTRUDO KALAMBAKAL
will be elected to serve on the FILIPINO ASSOCIATION. The
meeting will also study the ASSOCIATION's constitution and
discuss other problems in connection with the welfare of
Filipinos here.
Dr. KALAMBAKAL was born in SANTA CRUZ. LAGUNA in the
Philippines; he is 46 years old. He studied at the Japan
Medical College and took a post-graduate course in the Depart-
ment of Surgery at the Imperial University in TOKYO.
"I TH RED CROSS
During the First world War he worked with the Japanese
Rea Cross in TokYo, later continuing his studies in surgery
under Professor OCHSNER at the AUGUSTINE UNIVERSITY in America.
In 1922, together with the late Dr. SUN YAT SEN and other
siatic leaders, he became one of the founders of the Pan-
Asiatic ssociation, and also served as editor of the Asiatic
Review which was published oy the Association.
PRACTI SES HERE
During the Sino-Japanese hostilities in Shanghai in 1932
he joined the FOO MING HOSPITAL here and worked under Dr. TONGU
present heac of the Hospital.
Dr. KALaBAkiL later returned to the Philippines and star-
ted the PHILIFPINES EMANCIPATION MOVEMENT of which he became
Fresident and hed more than l000 followers.
Shortly before the outbreak of the GREATER EAST ASIA war,
Dr. KALAMBAKAL arrived in Shanghai end rejoined the FOO MING
HOSPITAL as surgeon."

On 19 April 1943, the following article appeared in the SHANGHAI
TIMES with the picture of the subject and other members of the
Filipino Community:
"KALAMBAKAL NAMED PRESIDENT OF FILIPINO ASSOCIATION IN
POLLS - OLD COMMI TTEE QUITS"
"Elected by an overwhelming majority, Dr. GERTRUDO KALAMBA
KAL, veteran in the fight for Philippine independence, was named
Fresident of the Filipino Association to succeed the outgoing
Mr. CONRADO A. UY, who, together with the provisionary committee,
SECRET

=== Page 174 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
GERTRUDO KALAMBAKAL
resigned at a meeting held at the LIDO BALLROOM yesterday
morning.
Immediately following his election, Dr. KALAMBAKAL. in
an interview with a representative of THE SHANGHAI TIMES
outlined the aims of the FILIPINO ASSOCIATION, stressing that
its main object is to co-operate with all Asiatic peoples. and
especially with Japan, as it is within the power of the latter
country to srant long-hoped for independence to the Philippines
and achieve the emancipation of the Filipino people.
Further. Dr. KALAMBAKAL declared, the ASSOCIATION will
strive to alleviate the condition of Filipinos here and start
relief work for the needy.
UY URGES CLOSER TIES
The elections were preceded by a speech delivered by
Lr. UY, outgoing president of the Association, who together
with the other provisional officers resigned en bloc at
yesterday's meeting.
Mr. UY declared that the time is now ripe for Filipinos
here to unite with the people at home to form a solid bloc
behind Japan and strive for independence. It is only through
unity, perseverance and trust in Japan that the independence
of the Philippines can be accomplished.
Taking the chair, Dr. KaLABAKAL enlarged upon the sen-
timents expressed by r. UY, saying that he will stand behind
whatever power which will rant Philippine independence. and
expressed the sincere belief that Japan at the successful
termination of the GREATER EAST ASIA WAR will grant autonomy
to the Filipino peoples, while voicin& gratitude to the
Japanese Government for its understanding of problems of
various Greater East Asie peoples.
LANDED WITH JAPANESE IN P.I.
Dr. KALAMBAKAL, who has during the past 10 years worked
for the independence of the Philippines, declared that he was
privileged to be with the Japanese forces when they landed in
the Philippines to drive out the Americans.
Dr. KALAMBAKAL further revealed that he has been able,
through his position, to instil confidence in the Filipino
people, laying great stress on the fact that the Japanese

The following is an extract from a signed statement made
on 15 October 1945 by CONRAD0 UY, leeding pro-Japanese Filipino
leader and propegandist :
"Dr. GERTRUDO KALAMBAKAL, a man without scruples, approached
me end proposed the reorganizetion of the ssociation on a radical
oasis. He wanted the organization to declare itself unequivoce-
bly pro-Japanese and effect the incarceration of detractors by
the Japanese Gendarmerie. He reveeled that he had made previous
consultations with certain Japanese Army officers who, he said,
were prepared to give any amount of financial help. Meanwhile,
he was stealthily telling other Filipinos, mostly musicians, that
he was authorized to set up a separate and altogether a different
body and that he would obtain the arrest of those blocking his
efforts. His surreptitious movement agained impetus with the sub-
sequent detention of two innocent musicians by the Japanese Gen-
darmerie supposedly at KALABAKAL's instigation.
Ly answer to this dangerous man's suggestion was clear and
precise: I would not object to any reorganization and general
election as long as ay name was dissociated from it. I abhorred
him as a man; as a self-styled leader he earned my bitter contempt.
I then consulted with my colleagues in the Association and they
readily shared my views and sentiments. We, therefore, decided
to resign en bloc should he succeed in calling a general meeting.
For the purpose of obtaining the necessary police permit to hold
the meeting. we designated CLARO ASUNCION to represent the board
of directors. t a subsequent meeting held at the LIDO BALLROOM
Medhurst Hoad. all the provisional officers of the Filipino A
sociation of Shanghai resigned and after making a few remarks.
I left the gathering with added anger and disgust because I di
covered that LlaldnkAl had sent out threatening letters
(Ehibit 3) to the members which fact was responsible for the
record attencance. I must state at this juncture that I have
always tried to avoic any movement that employs force or intimi-
dation as a weapon to accomplish its aims and purposes. From then
on I kept aloof from KAlAMBAKAL end his cohorts. Of what happened
later in the LIPUANG PILIPINO, as the new body was called, I
kmow very little. Aside from entertaining Japanese wounded
soldiers in a military hospital and cavorting with a number of
Japanese officers, the LIPUNANG PILIPINO accomplished nothing.
And like its precursor, it died a natural death. KALAMBAKAL
was later found to heve used the Association only as a stepping
stone to TOKYO, leaving the organization under JOSE C. CONTRERAS."

The following sined statement was made on 23 October.
1945 by A. NAVARRO :
"I. LFONSO NAVARRO. make this statement of my own free
Will. On or about the beginning of March, 1943, Dr. KALAMBAKAL
came to see me in my house and told me that he wanted me to
sign a paper to ask for the Philippine independence and relief
to Filipinos fron the Japanese. Then he handed me the paper
and told me to end the other boys that were also present in my
house at that time to read it. Among those that were present
and read the paper were the following: J. CANLAS, F. DIAZ. E.
MARTINEZ, F. SAÍVADOR, T. CRUZ, nd M. SANTILLAN. CANLAS, DIAZ
and MARTINEZ refused to sign the petition.
KALAMBAKAL was a frequent visitor at my house and almost
every night at the dancing hall where I play. I remember KALAM-
BakiL having told me once at the dancing hall that he will do
his best to have Dr. EVANGELISTA taken away from the office of
the SiISS CONSULATE and to be replaced by another Filipino. He
has also told me that he is also a member of the Pan Asiatic
Association which is working for the independence of the
Philigpines. He has also showm me written articles written in
Spanish and Tagalog advocating Philippine independence and these
articles are supposed to have been published in Manila papers.
He asked me to help him make the Filipinos understand his good
intentions for our country and ask the Japanese for our indepen-
dence. I answered "yes".
One day, KaLaiBAkAL met me on my way home and tol me to
come with him and the other boys to tea at the TRICOLOR where
he will tell us where A. ROSETE was. With us were S. FRANCISCO
T. CRUZ, D. BALDEVARONA. During tea we telk about common topics.
After tea KALAMBAkAL invited us to take the motor car which was
parked outside of the TRI COLOR and drove us to No.1O Avenue
Petain, Gendarmerie Headquarters.

- I -
FILIPINO MUSICIANS UNION
versus
FILIPINO ASSOCIATION
On pril 17th, 1942 a Preliminary Meeting was held in my house
in reponse to the complaints of many musicians regarding compulsion
under threat to join the FILIPINO ASSOCIATION and to play at the
Micnight Bell scheduled to take place at the PARADISE BALLROOM on
April 25th, 1942. In the evening of April 17th Mr. JOSE C. CONTRERAS
asked me to explain why I was forming another organization against
the FILIPINO ASSOCIATION which was already formed for the whole
Filipino Community under the protection of the Japanese. Mr. CONTRE
RAS accused me of persuading the musicians not to play at the proposed
Lidnight Ball which affair was cancelled owing to a telephone call
received by the CENTRAL POLICE STATION advising them that trouble
would occur if the ball was held. Consequently the affair was
cancelled. The FILIPINO MUSICIANS UNION was formally inaugurated on
May 1st,1942, and in the evening of May 12th I was assaulted by
Messrs. ALFONSO NAVARRO and CONTRERAS and dismissed from work by Mr.
C.NTRERAS immediately. F.M.U. members increased inspite of
Mr. CONTRERAS' instructions to all musicians not to join this pro-
Anerican orgenization, the F.M.U.

The second day I was feč two meels put not questioned. The
third day I was questioned agein (HIkkO) and asked: "Why did

VALENTINE PUNSALAN - l027 House 70 Broadway.
1.
The subject made the following signed statem at on 15 C ober 1945:
I. VALENTINE PUNSALAN am making this statement of my own free will.
Late in 1943 I was visited at my home by a Japanese called M. Takkn
The first few visits he talked in a friendly manner and brought presents
for the children. From this conversation I gathered that he thought that
I was a pro-Japanese Filipino. Thus he was very friendly towards me and
said that he was going to fix it up so that I could get a loan. In fact.
he stated that he was going to get all pro-Japanese Filipinos loans. Some
time after these talks TAkEDA called on me three times and each time I
was out. The third time he left a note asking me to call at his office on
the eighth floor of the Foreign Y.M.C.A. I therefore called at this office.
TAKEDA asked me to give him information on members of the Filipino commu-
nity who were pro-American and who might cause trouble. He said that he
wanted information on Dr. H. EVANGELISTA, P.C. ESPINO or any other
Filipino who might feel as they did. I stated that I did not know any-
thing about these men; he then said that I must know something about them.
Our conversation continued for about half an hour as we discussed various
matters in a friendly way. Some time between two and three in the after-
noon someone knocked on the door end J. ALIND. (alias DON JOSE) came
into the room. He appeared very surprised to ste me. The three of us
telked for some time in a friendly way and I t lieve DON JOsE was convinced
by TAkEna's manner that I was pro-Japanese anć herefore could be trusted.
After some time TAKEDA asked DON JOSE: "What is Dr. EVANGELISTA?".
DON JOSE replied: "ell, this guy is pro-American and he is always
making propaganda for Americans. He is the one who gave advice to some
Filipinos not to attend the PAN-ASIATIC ATHELETIC MEET at the RACE COURSE.
He is working against our organization. EVANGELISTA is their leader and
ESPIKO is helping him. MATEOS and some other Filipinos are mixed up in
this too. I think that we must take these people out of the way."
The subject was then shifted to counteract the effect on the
Filipinos of the relief that they are receiving from the SWISS CONSULATE.
DON JOSE therefore suggested that they should put a notice in the paper
that a relief from the PHILIPPINES would be given to Filipinos though
they know positively no relief could come from this direction. Mr.
TAkED then offered us tea and cakes and he left the room and during that
time DON JOSE said to me: (in a whisper) "This must be between you and me,
don't let anybody know about this meeting or that you have seen me here.
We are now here, we ere Filipinos, and everybody wants to live and don't
forget that this is an occupied area and they can do anything they want
with us."
At this point Mr. TAkEDA re-entered the room and after having coffee
we left his office. As stated above I heard DON JOSE advise Mr. TAKEDA
that he thought the best way to counteract the good news of Allied loans
through the SWISS CONSULATE to the Filipinos was to publish a statement in
the newspapers that relief hed arrived from the LAUREL Government. DON
JOSE stated that of course this was not true but it would be good propa-
SECRET

=== Page 206 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
o2.
VALENTINE PUNSALAN - 1027 House 70 Broadway
ganda in the papers. TAKEDA said that this sounded like a good idea
to him.
Some time after 27 February 1944, the date EVANGELISTA, MATEOS
ESPINO and CRUZ, JAVIER, VILLA were arrested TAKEDA called at my
place of work. COMLUCK RESTAURANT and asked me about E. LORENZO.
He said: "How about this man LORENZO? Is he doing any talking
against the Japanese to the boys in the band or to other Filipinos,
because so far I heard that he is a big mouth always talking against
Japanese." I replied that as far as I knew LORENZO was not talking
about anything about politics. Mr. TAkEDA then stated: "I am ready
to take LORENzo tonight, what do you think about. do you think it
will spoil your bano?" I replied: "Well, I can 't say yes or no to
you, this action is up to you, but don't forget as I told you the
first time what he has been talking about and also don't forget that
he is a sick man." TAkEDA then said: "Well, I will think it over.
I'll leave hin alone this time. I will find out something more
about him." Then TAKEDa left me.
Previous to TAkEDA's visits a Lieutenant MUKOYAMA of the
Japanese Gendarmerie called on me. He said that since I )VALENTINE
PUNSALAN) wes merried to a Japanese woman naturally I must
cooperate with the Jap nese. He asked me what I knew about the
Filipino community in general and whet I knew in particular about
.Y. CELIs ald what kind of man he was, and what was doing in the
MUSICIANS UNION as well as the Filipino community. I replied that
I dian't kmow much about what was going on. kUKOYAnA then changed
the subject and said: "I know you are not making much money, if
you want to make extra business here is what you should do. Report
to me on what you hear outside avout mericans and other Allies in
town. Report to me if you see anyone giving signals during black-
outs." He then gave me the name of some foreigner (which I do not
now recall) and said that he was suspected of being a spy. He seid
that he wented me to check up on this man for him and get informa-
tion. I replied that I woulo try but I took no further action on
the matter. He left me after advising me to turn in any information
to his office on Canton Road. Some time later I met MUkOYAMA on
the street and told him I was having housing trouble. He said to
call at his office and I did so. I was greatly surprised to see
MUKOYAMA appeer in an army uniform, with officer's insignia as I
thought that he was just a plain service man. We had a converse-
tion and he told me that he was going to fix my house. Nothing
came of it; that was the last time that I saw Lieutenant MUkoYAMA.
In conclusion I wish to go on record as stating that I was
never guilty of actively aiding the Japanese. Because I spoke their
language they thought that I would aid them, but I never did.
(signed) V. PUNSALAN.

I was married and have two children - all in
Shanghai. I got separated with my wife and two children
in 1942, due to financial difficulties because of the out-
break of Pacific War. I have a monthly income of US$600.00
or Pesos 1,200.00 before the war in Shanghai from my com-
pany.
Subsistence and maintenance for myself, wife and
two children comes from the "American Relief Committee of
the American Association of Shanghai," my personal guaran-
tor an American business-man named R. S. BoYD - owner of
the "NORTH AMERICAN SYNDICATE", 41O SZECHUEN Road, Shanghai
for the relief. Later our relief was transferred. Came
from the Swiss Consulate up to present.

ARMANDO J. SALES (Continued):
I could say I am loyal to my government "Common-

owe only an allegiance to the above-mentioned govern-

sign up papers. Also second proof, I quite remember
and signed, and declared myself before the Swiss Consul
that I was an enemy of Japan. All these things
happened as the final showdown for us Filipinos here in
Shanghai. That was the time when we Filipinos here
became divided into two parties.
As a loyal citizen of my country, I only request
and suggest that each and everyone of those who separated
from our old government and have sworn into the "Puppet
Japanese Philippine Government," be asked any questions
before - I suggest and what mostly of us here wants. A
Compulsory Meeting of all Filipinos to be given in a desig-
nated place you may desire. I knew my countryman very
well - talk too much but nothing happened. Besides of
what I always hear is that it's in every one of those
who, whom we called "YES MEN" have his or her own personal
reasons and they said they would like to voice it out
before us. So what I think those ideas are very fine.
As to your request that I help the government to find out
those Bad Filipinos here, this meeting will be a nice
place to hear and see for you and for me, who are the real
ones what we call criminals.
As to the meetings you have been asking me, I might
as well say what I see, heard and been to:
On or about January 5, 1942, a meeting was called
for all Filipinos through newspaper. Held at METROPOLE
Ballroom. Present: All Filipinos and Japanese high
officers. An association names "Filipino Association of
Shanghai" was formed - motto: Relief and mutual benefit
for the Community. President: CONRADO A. UY. (Doubting:
We know he is half-caste Chinese and he is Filipino, but
some say he's got Chinese Passport.) Mysterious. Know
him only during meeting war.Later, he published a news-
paper called "FILIPINO CLARION" making propaganda. I
think so as to what I have read. I'm sorry I have not
got one. Some of my countrymen have them. They are
only being given to members. I was not and never been a
member even if this was a community organization. I was
very busy myself struggling for my daily needs.
On or about April 18, 1943, a mysterious man named
Dr. G. KALAMBAKAL appeared in Shanghai - issued to all
Filipinos a threatening letter, forcing all of us to attend
that meeting. Present: Filipinos and Japanese High

all about us. Lucky they did not harm us, only they made
a Force Closing of the U. S. Government Relief Loan for us
in the Swiss Consulate from August, 1944, up to this peace
time. They made us hungry and some of us died of starva-
tion. I was able to manage by making and opening a "Second-
hand Commission Open Air Storen in the streets of Shanghai,
travelling from one street to another. This is what we
called in the States - Junk Business.
Lastly, I suggest and request the United States
Government through your kind office or its representatives

The following signed statement was submitted by the
SUBJECT on 22 Qctober, 1945:
"Shanghai, Oct. 22, 1945.
I, F. SALVADOR, make this statement of my own
free will.
In March 1943 KALANBAKAL invited me to tea also
present were ALFONSO NAVARRO, DEMETRIO VALDEVARONA
and T. CRUZ. After tea we got in a car and I was
surprised when we arrived at the AMERICAN SCHOOL
and were taken into a room with Japanese in uniform.
KALANBAKAL spoke in Japanese to these men and I did
not understand what he said.
KALANBAKAL spoke to us in Tagalog and instructed
us to tell the Japanese about DR. EVANGELISTA and
other pro-American Filipinos. The Japanese showed
me a picture of Dr. E. and I said I knew him. They
asked me if he was a spy and I did not answer yes or
no. I can't remember what the others answered.
The Japanese held a small piece of paper in his
hand as he asked us about the Filipinos and whether
we knew of any Filipinos who are pro-American and
making propaganda against the Japanese.
DR. KALANBAKAL then took this piece of paper and
said: "Come on we better sign this paper in case
we need some other pass or papers and then the Japa-
nese will know us and we can obtain from them easily."
Then he handed each of us the paper and we signed.
Then we went home. KALANBAKAL, VALDEVARONA and
I think NAVARRO went back the next day but I don't
know what they did.
In June 1943 KALANBAKAL brought a petition to me
to sign saying that if we signed it we would have
some unity and we could send to Tokyo for some help.
It was on LIPUNANG PILIPINO letterhead and started:
SECRET

DR. EVANGELISTA, ALFREDÓ MATEOS, MARIANO, JAVIER,

of the following: (1) For being one of the leaders

a secret office in the SWISS CONSULATE.' (3) For
receiving instructions from WASHINGTON thru the SWISS
CONSULATE.
(4) For making periodical reports to the
SWISS CONSULATE regarding the Filipinos who were co-
operating with JAPAN. (5) For thwarting all pro
Japanese demonstrations by the Filipinos. (6) For
circulating rumors that the Filipinos who would attend
the Philippine Independence celebrations will have
their loan stopped. (7) For taking pictures during
the independence celebrations. (8) For not recogniz-
ing the LAUREL GOVERNMENT and instructing other Fili-
pinos not to recognize said government. (9) For dise-
minating news and ideas derogatory to Japan.
(10) For
using my house as a center of propaganda."

"October 25, 1945.
About a few days after, I was taken out of my
cell and brought to a room where I saw DR. KALAMBAKAL,
D. BALDEVARONA, A. NAVARRO, F. SALVADOR, T. CRUZ and
the Jap investigator, all sitting around a big round
table.
BALDEVARONA brought out DR. EVANGELISTA'S picture
in military uniform and said, "Here is your American
Chief's picture." I answered, "He is our Chief."
Then A. NAVARRO told me that they came there because
they wanted to take me out of jail on condition that
I must tell the Jap that I have been telling DR. EVAN-
GELISTA news about Japanese and Filipinos in Shangha1
and that I do not cooperate with the Japanese because
the Americans will come back.
I was wondering what their intention was for they
were forcing me to say the above statement. As the
above conversation was in Tagalog, the Jap told us
that we should talk in English. I was silent all
the while because I was thinking what to say. Then
the Jap told me to say all what I know about DR. EVAN-
GELISTA. I kept quiet. Then A. NAVARRO told me
that if I would not say what they wanted me to say to
the Japanese, I could not come out of jail. The Jap
told me that he must investigate me again. A. NAVARRO
and his gang left telling me not to forget what they
told me to say so that I could be released. I did

EVALUATION: C-3.
The following is an extract from a signed statement
made by P. C. ESPINO regarding his arrest:
"Shanghai, 16th October, 1945.
I heard that the Japanese were watching me around
February 1943 and I was arrested 14 March 1943 by
three Japanese gendarmes (including HIRANO who was in
charge). I was taken to BRIDGE HOUSE after they
searched my house and seized all documents and pictures.
I was not allowed to talk or say goodbye to my family.
I was questioned by HIRANO and the others at home
and at BRIDGE HOUSE. They asked: "Do you know
EVANGELISTA, J. CANLAS, A. ROSETA, S. TOLEDO (nOW
deceased)? What did you do last Christmas? (they
were suspicious of my part in raising funds for Xmas
dinner for war prisoners)."
At BRIDGE HOUSE they examined my papers but found
nothing incriminating. I was then put in confinement
for the night.
The second day I was fed two meals but not questioned.
The third day I was questioned again (HIRAnO) and asked:
"Why did you refuse to join the (Japanese sponsored)
Filipino Association?"
On the paper he held while he questioned me I noticed
DR. KALANBAKAL'S signature. He showed me this paper.
He then asked me why I solicited Filipinos to get
funds for the dinner. He showed me this paper also and
it was signed by VALDEVARONA.
Another paper he showed me was an accusation sighed
by FRANCISCO SALVADOR - "Why did you induce Filipinos
to repatriate to the United States?"
The Salvador paper also accused me applications for
repatriation from Filipinos.
SECRET

SECRET
F. SALVADOR
An accusation signed by FRANCISCO SAMPER stated
that I and EVANGELISTA held political meetings in
my home. This was not true although I did have
many Filipinos call at my home for advice.
An accusation by A. ORDEN said that I refused
to sign the petition for (puppet) Filipino independence
(true).
Most of these things I admitted I had done but I
stated that these were my duties and I had done nothing
wrong.
They tried to make me admit I had held political
meetings and when I denied this I was slapped and
beaten by HIRANo for one and a half to two hours.
Then I was returned to my cell, where I spent 7 days
before being questioned again."
EVALUATION: C-3.
The following signed statements were made by JOSE
CANLAS regarding his arrest on 19 March, 1943:
"Shanghai, October 1, 1945.
A week after my arrest, I was taken out of the
cell for investigation. The first question I was
asked was if I loved my country. I said I did.
The second was if I liked the Americans. I said I
did and I got slapped. They asked me if I knew
the following perSons: G. KALAMBAKAL, ALFONSO NAVARRO,
D. BALDEVARONA, FRANCISCO SALVADOR and TIRSO CRUZ.
Then like a flash it came to my mind that these Fili-
pinos were the ones that turned informer to the
Japanese. After that they read to me the charge. I
was charged of being anti Japanese, American propagan-
dist and spy. I was so dazed for it was not too easy
to be called a spy. In support of this charge they
read to me affidavit signed by the persons mentioned
above. KALAMBAKAL charged me for refusing to join
the FILIPINO ASSOCIATION, a Japanese sponsored organ
for disrupting Filipino loyalty to the United States.
That I refused to sign the association's petition for
Philippine Independence. BALDEVARONA charged me for
being a spy receiving instruction from MR. HALE through
DR. EVANGELISTA. A. NAVARRO charged me in disemina-
ting ideas and transmitting instructions to pro American
Filipinos. FRANCISCO SALVADOR charged me for going
among the Filipinos instructing them not to sign the
independence petition. TIRSO CRUZ charged me with
gathering informations and submitting them to DR. EVAN-
GELISTA. I denied all the charges. I have been

VICTORINO TULIO
The following signed statement was made on 16 November 1945
by the subject :
"There was one Association headed by UY and some notable
Filipinos when I came to SHANGHAI. I was approached by Mr. CONRADO
UY, the President of the union to support him in his political aims
for which I flatly refused since I am not interested in any campaign
politically.
After the occupation of P.I. by the Japs, came KALAMBAKAL who
claims himself the Angel of the Philippines; boasted of his connec-
tions with High Military Command in SHAnGHAI and elsewhere and who
threatened and forced up to attend meetings and signed petitions to
the Jap's government for something that I could not remember. Prior
to this, some of our Filipino boys were rounded up, brought to the
Bridge house for detention and Investigation and who did it was far
beyond my knowledge. As to my personal opinion, these incidents
brought forth hatred, jealousy and revenge among the Filipinos.
One of the co-campaigners and propagandist in the UY group by
the name of VICENTE CAPINO who dislike the American rule in the
Philippines said, "Once and for all the American should be extermi-
nated in P.I."
Being the husband of a Japanese girl for 14 years and to save
the situation for my family and skip all troubles, I did answer yes.
I regret to say that should there be no Filipinos that colla
borated with the Jap and stooges that spilled things in the SWISS
CONSULATE. split and disorder won't occur and all Filipinos here
would be solid and firm in their co-operative movements.
Witnesses:
(signeā) VICTOR TULIO.

broken by a great disillusionment. For a whole week I was
spiritually depressed and I could not sleep. I wanted to keep
the secret and told Tria to keep it at first, but later I felt
it was my duty to let it out to the rest of our comnunity.
Throwing discretion to winds, I started retailing the stories to
the musicians and the news spread like wildfire. At this
juncture, a number of Japanese hewsmen wanted my comnent on
Gen. McArthur's landings in Lingayen, but I flatly refused to
make any statement. They became suspicious. I became a
marked man. About a week later a Japanese friend tipped me that
a report was received by the Japanese Gendarmerie on my alleged
anti-Japanese activities since my resignation from The Shanghai
Times and warned me to be very careful. But nothing happened
for thereafter I kept my mouth shut much as I id not like to.
During the following montns up to Aug. ll, I realized that I
became persona non rata to the Japanese authorities because
they never even once asked me to do anything for them.
In giving you an account of my disillusionment brought
about by Japanese uplicity and mpty pretensions of magnanimity
and scuare deal, it is not my intention to whitewash my previous
acts but to point out the fact that the sense of realism that
had prompted me to applaud Japan's policy towi rd the Philippines
vas the same sense of realism that later impelled me to renounce
my co-operation  ith them and revise my concepts and beliefs
about their avowed policies. The ideals involved have never
changed - they are Tixed and steadfast as the stars. The issue
of Philippine independence is still awaiting just solution
from the Unit d Sta ues  mile she e lancipation of other Asiatic
nations, oopresse nd tr uuc a  or centuries by iaperialistic

=== Page 235 === (confidence 0.98)
Page 10.
forces, is still in the lap of the gods. Even confirmed pro-
American postles like rigadier-General Carlos P. Romulo, who
is now in Washington, could not ignore the inexorable realities
of a chan ed world. In a recent statement carried by U.P. he
said, "Japan's call for a co-prosperity sphere is still basi-
cally v lid and morally unassail ble ue to the exploitation of
European powers, but the J panese failed due to their crudity."
The United States of America today is the leacing country
in all the  orld. She i: the enbodiment of power, the biggest
res rvoir of wea th, the rountain source of robust democratic
ideals and the protector of weak ations. Will she allow pre-
Victorian impericlism to tirottle tle athering movenent of
nationslism in Est tsia? ill he allow European powers to
continue their exploitation o the eaxer nations in the East?
Will she help supject peoples to obtain their emancipation from
orein omination? The Asiatics want to know.
Si ned: CONRADO UY.
October 15, 1945."

THE JAPANESE SOUTH CHINA INTELLIGENCE GROUP FIVE PROVINCES INFORMATION DEPT.
1. HISTORY
THE SOUTH CHINA INTELLIGENCE GROUP deyToped in 194O as a product of the
Japanese espionage leader General DoIHARA Kenjiro who is the founder of
the present Japanese plainclothes espionage system.
In l94O General DOIHARA sent an agent Major General OkUBO to Canton to
establish an espionage system for the five provinces of Southern China.
OKUBO establ shed his headquarters at 26 Pearl Road Central, Canton,
but received his orders fron the Japanese Puppet Secret Service Headquar-
ters in Nanking. At first this new agency was to be a section of the
Nanking Puppet Central Covernment Propaganda Department which is headed
in Nanking by LIN Po Sheng, a very good friend of General OkUBO. About
two months after its founding, this new agency became known as the SouTH
CHINA INTELLICENCE GROUP FIVE PROVINCES IMFORMATION DEPARTMENT. JaPIn-
ese supervihion was still maintained, however, through the Puppet Secret
Service Headguarters in Nanking. The part played by LIN Po Sheng in
this SOUTH CHINA INTELLIGENCE GROU: is not clear.
With the esteblishment of central headquarters at No. 26 Pearl Road, the
following brench offices were cstablished:
Shanghai -
26 Szechuan Road Central
Sha Ping -
Room No. 22, Golden Gate Hotel
Hong Kong -
Fu Cheong Trading Company
Macau -
No. 34 Man Wai Road (The Asia Trading Company)
Macau -
11th Floor, Central Hotel
Kwangchouwan - Room /206, South China Hotel
Kwangchouwan - Fu Cheong Trading Company
The main wireless station was located on the third flcor of the Canton
Headquarters. A branch wireless station which is believed to be the
receiving station is located on the 6th floor of the Aoi Chun Hotel in
Canton. A recreation hall which is used as a meeting place is located
at 27 Pearl Road Central, Canton, just across the street from the main
headquarters.
At prosent, information regarding 26 lmown agents and seven suspected
agents of the SOUTH CHINA INTE LIGENCE GROUP has been received.
Available information indicated at least four main sections to the SOUTH
CHINA INTELLIGENCE GROUP to be as follows:
HEADQUARTERS SECTION
Major Ceneral OKUBO in charge
ESPIONAGE SECTION
CHAN Chi Kong in charge 東 子光
COMMUNICATIONS SECTION
TSUN Chi Kong in charge 秦 志
PROPAGANDA SECTION
"FATTY" LAU in charge
There is evidence also of a probable Counterespionage Section which
worked directly under Major General OKUBO and which may have been headed
by LI Pei Lo, a woman who has been arrested by the Chinese authorities
and who is detained at present somewhere in Kwangsi Province.

II. IEADQUARTERS SECTION
The headquarters section of the SOUTH CHINA INTELLIGENCE GROUP estab-
lished its main office at 26 Pearl Road Central, Canton. Seven kmom
branch offices were established in addition to two known radio stations
1
and a recreation hall.
Mejor General CKUBO, FOK Wing Kai and WOK Wing Kan are known to be the
main leaders. In addition, LI Pei Lo, who is intimate with OKUBO, is
believed to have been used as a check upon the other agents and as a
counterespionage head to combat allied penetration. Her position in the
espionage section is not clear and for that reason she will be considered
under the Headquarters Section.
Available information of the officials of the Headquarters Section is as
follows:
OKUBO, Vajor Ceneral, Japanese Army
Subject is a graduate of the Japanese Officers' Infantry Academy in Tokyo.
Shortly after his graduation he was appointed an army division commander
by KoNOE. After the l931 incident he was sent to Manchuria to organize
a Japanese Secret Service under the direction of General DOIHARA Kenjiro.
In 1940 he was sent to Canton by DOIHARA to organize a Japanese Secret
Service. Under DOIHARA's direction he organized the SOUTH CHINA INTELLI-
GENCE GROUP FIVE PROVINCES INFORIHATION DEPARTMENT to cover the five
southern provinces of Kwangtung, Kwangsi, Hunan, Fukien, and Anhwei. He
established the headquarters for this organization at No. 26 Pearl Road
Central, Canton.

LI Pei Lo (Chinese, female) 李 比露
Subject arrived in Shanghai from Tientsin in 194l and remained for six
months. She then went to llong Kong where she worked for a Japanese
firm nemed the Fu Cheong Trading Company. This firm was the Hong Kong
office for the SOUTH CHINA INTELLIGENCE CROUP. In October 1942 she was
sent to Kweilin where she lived in Room No. 37 of the Paris Hotel, a
room frequently used by suspected agents in Kweilin.
On 10 September 1942 she was arrested by TAI Li agents in Kweilin. A
search of her room revealed two identification cards issued to her by
the Fu Cheong Trading Company in Hong K ng. Many inquiries have been
made into subject's case, but to dato the Chinese have not executed her.
Her position is somewhat obscure. ubject claims to have come from
Hupeh Province where her father was born, but she has no living relatives
in Free China. Subject's mother is Australian and living in Australia
at the present time. There were two entries in her diary which indicated
that she was not a native of China. The first entry stated, "When I
arrived in Shanghai I then understood the greatness of it. I saw all the
beautiful and great buildings which I had heard about." Another entry
stated, "By the introduction of one of my friends to a Mr. CHAN, a
Chinese friend, and after several talks with him, I felt that the Chinese
were very lovely and kind, so made me love him."
Informants believe subject to be a Formosan schooled in Japan. Subject's
connection with the SOUTH CHINA INTELLIGENCE GROUP is not clear. She
is an intimate friend of General OKUBO. Informants believe that subject
was used as a check on other members of the group and also as a counter-
espionage agent.
At present subject is detained somewhere in Kwangsi Protince by the
Chinese officials - probably in Pai-se (Poseh).

direct supervision of CHAN Chi Kong.
Agents were employed who were familiar with the areas to which they were
to be sent.
Available information indicates nine Inown agents of this section and
two suspected agents. Associates of the lmown and suspected agents are
not included and will be handled in a later section.
Details of the members of this section are as follows:
CILAN Chi Kong (Chinese) 陳子光
Subject is the net controller and director of the espionage section of
the SOUTH CHINA INTELLIGENCE GROUP.

CHAN Wai Hung (Chinese - executed) 陳惠共
Subject was an a gent for the SOUTH CHINA INTELLIGENCE GROUP and was under

Subject made a full confession which has been instrumental in placing

admitted that he was sent to Kweilin by CHAN Chi Kong of the SOUTH CHINA
