VII. OPERATIONAL DATA
There has been little data obtainable regarding the operations of the
SOUTH CHINA INTELLIGENCE GROUP.
For convenience, the available operational date is divided as follows:
1.
Hission
2.
Personnel
3.
Recruiting
4.
Training Schools
5.
Training
6.
Priority
7.
Pay
8.
Cover
9.
Comnunications
10.
Group Finance
11.
Codes
1. Mission
The mission of the SOUTH CHINA INTELLIGENCE GROUP is one primarily of
espionage and morale operations. There has been no indication of
sabotage or assassination activities on the part of known agents,
although the training schools include demolition instruction in their
schedules.
From available information, the Group missi on is as follows:
Report on - Military movements in the South China War Zones.
American Air Force and the locations and strength of fighter
and bomber bases.
Intelligence organization of the Allied forces.
Quartermaster depots, torage godowms, and supply dumps.
- Allied camouflage systems.
- Conditions and morale of Allied forces.
In addition, agents of the Group are instructed to carry the doctrines
of Puppet Government and to further the meaning of the Co-prosperity
Sphere.
2. Personnel
The headquarters staff of the SOUTH CHINA INTELLIGENCE GROUP is composed
primarily of educated Chinese puppets who have at one time or another
studied in Japan. Available information indicates that the staff may

=== Page 266 === (confidence 0.99)
- 15 -
have at one time been indoctrinated in the Japanese nationalistic ideals
while they were in Japan.
The group leaders or agents appear to be recruited from the tough class
of gangsters and gang laaders in Canton and Hong Kong. Cantonese
Chineso predominate and most of the leaders speak English.
The informants and lesser agents are recruited from the lower class
gangsters, doctors, backward military officers, dissatisfied politicians,
fortune tellers, loafers, and prostitutes.
3. Recruiting
Available information indicates that SOUTH CHTIA GROUP agents are re-
cruited only through personal contact with members. A prospective agent
is reported to the group and he is watched. Propaganda is fed to him by
a trusted friend who is a member of the group in order that his reactions
and sympathies may be observed. If the prospect is found to be favorable,
he is first placed on duty wi th the puppet police force so that his
abilities may be watched and he may be more completely indoctrinated. He
is then asked to work for the Group and sent to the training school.
Only in the case of agent PAI Chi has there been any indication of coercion
and intimi dation used in the recruiting or employment of agents.
4. Training Schools
The training schools for the SOUTH CHINA INTELLIGENCE GROUP change their
locations quite often. At present the main training school is located
at the old Police Club on Tung Ming Road in Canton. Previously this
school was held at both the Wampoa Military Academy and the old Chinese
Air Force Club. This main school is knon as the Special ervice Train-
ing Academy.
5. Training Procedure
Special Training is given from three to six months. If the student is
capable, he may graduate in the minimum time of three months.
Subjects taught include dialects, custams and habits of localities, pro-
cedure of interrogation and cross examination, methods of communications,
ideals of cover, and puppet indoctrination. Instruction is also given
in the identification and use of Allied weapons and radio operation and
repair is teught to some. Instructors are mostly puppet officials who are
Japanese returned students. Some Japanese intelligence officers are used
as instructors for special courses.
The president of the training branch of the SOUTH CHINA INTELLIGENCE GROUP
is Lt. Colonel KUO Wei Ming who is the Chief Commander of the Police
Department of the Puppet Kwangtung Province.
SECRET

=== Page 267 === (confidence 0.96)
6. Priority
Agents are eppointed to an area either just in advance of Japanese attacks
or right after. Long-term agents are sent to areas of military impor-
tance for deep penetration. Agents in occupied areas have the right to
control opium, gambling, prostitution, and to smuggle within certain areas.
7. pay
Agent's pay is from 200 dollars Jap Military yuan to 800 Jap Military
Yuan. Arents are allowed to smuggle and squeeze. For certain operations,
large sums are given in either gold or silver. CHAn Wai Hung was paid
9000 in silver plus 10 taels of gold in addition to his regular salary
of 800 Military Yuan. Informants are paid $40 to $150 Jap Military Yuan.
8. Cover
The main cover used hy leaders and agents in free China is that of a
business man or doctor. Lesser agents and informants take the cover of
fortune tellers or refugees. All agents enter Free China as smugglers.
Couriers back to Cccupied areas use the smuggling cover.
9. Communications
Communications are usually carried out by courier or runner carrying
coded messages in the form of letters. An agent very seldom leaves Free
China but goes near the border using a smuggling or running merchant
cover. Near the border he contacts certain gangsters who usually carry
the information across the border as smugglers.
10. Group Finance
The finances for the SOUTH CHINA INTELLIGENCE CROUr are derived primarily
from puppet levies. Additional finance is received from goods smuggled.
11. Codes
Little information is available regarding codes used by the SOUTH CHINA
INTELLIGENCE GROUP agents in Free China. Available information indicates
that codes made from commercial products were used however.
The known codes are as fcllows:
Large size tubes of toothpaste indicate B-29's
Small

Troops are referred to as metal. Numbers of troops are referred to by the
numbers 1 to 9 indicating thousands. This is indicated by an intercepted
letter which read "Mr. WoNG bought the metal which you requested and left
Liuchow for Kweiyang on the 2oth instant." This was decoded to read General
WONG departed Liuchow for Kweiyang with 20,O00 troops.

SUPPLEMENTARY INFORIATION TO KWEIYANG X-2 REPORT DATED 2O AUGUST 1945 ON

SCURCE: Drafter.
The South China Five Provinces Intelligence Office is under the
control of the Nanking  ang Regime. After the loss of Kweilin, its name
was changed to the South China Political Peace Preservation Board. About
70% of its finance was supplied by the Puppet Government through the
Ministry of Propagande. The remaining 30% was from Jap military sources.
From authorization given to them, no other organization, civil or mili-
tary, had power to interfere or arrest any of its members.
The main headquarters was located in Nenking. Its South China head
office was located at 26 Pearl Piver Road (海 王朱路) and there was a
glub at the Ci Kwan Hotel on the 6th floor. The above room at Oi Kwan
was rented by CHAN Pak Yung (陳 伯 容) and used as a false radio station.
Actually, all information was handled at the Pearl River Road address.
The organization in Canton was formed by YUN Yan (熊 容). His assis-
tants were WoNG Kee (汪 紀) and LEE it Tsung (李式 曾). This or-
ranization was divided into six sub-groups and they were as follows:
Intelligence
Counter Espionage
Political Intelligence
Training
Finance and Administration
Each of these sub-departments was headed by a Chinese. The chief
of the organization changed annually. nce 1940, the successive chiefs
were:

This organization had little or nothing to do, as far as exchange
of information is concerned, with any of the other Jap intelligence
groups. They had liaison with the Navy and the Army mainly for the pur-
pose of getting priority in communications and transportation. Their
work wi th the Japanese Consulate was also limited, however, they did
report any Japanese or Formosans who might have had revolutionary inten-
tions. The type of information that they sought was military targets
of Allied Air Forces in China, political changes, economic changes,
diplomatic relations and Allied intentions that might effect China. They
were also interested in the Reds.

Te training rogram undertaken by this organization was handled by
HO Yan an (叫 人 云). Each class, composed of about 33 persons, was
trained for a period of three months. The classes were held every night
for a period of l hours. No one was excused unless he had been assigned
to sane specific duty. The training consisted of lectures and few demon-
strations on the use of arms, and particularly the recognition of Allied
equipment. One of the lecturers was lirs. LEE who has been one of the most
prominent Jap arents in the Gendarmerie since the War. She is a Jap and
her name is IMAI (今 # ). She is 413", 40 years old. Her former address
was Kwong Tap Road，Peach Lane, 61 (光±答路杏花巷 61 號後通。 The
school was their headquarters on Pearl Road. Most of the students were
uneducated individuals such as detectives, petty gangsters, etc. After
training each of the graduates, could have 10 followers upon authority
given. Because these agents were uneducated, they were compelled to make
their reports personally or by going to the nearest Jap or Naval seoret
radio station or by sending verbal reports by courier back to headquarters.
According to the source, there is a considerable number of "stay
behind" agents left in Free China. These agents have no definite assigm-
i . t but are to report whatever information they can get..
The radio station is mostly handled by Shanghainese and except by
£ cial permission, they were not allowed to travel freely. They were
isolated from other members of the organization and were kept under sur-
veillance by the Gendarmerie Secret Police.
Further report on this organization will be availab le on the com-
pletion of the Marlin Mission. Many members of the organization are still
free in Macau where they enjoy comparative safety. Not until these mem-
bers are brought in for questioning will there be a true picture of this
organization.

=== Page 277 === (confidence 0.96)
MEMORANDUM
RE:
Hajimu MASUDA
According to his own statements, Hajimu MASUDA was born in
Los Angeles, California, on 8 June 1915, the son of Kiyonobu and
Haruno MASUDA. He was issued an American passport in June 1937 and
in the same month left for Japan where he arrived on 28 June 1937.
Although MASUDA did not return to the United States, he claims that
he has never renounced his American citizenship. He claims to have
been forced to join the Japanese Army (in October 1942) and to have
had Japanese citizenship conferred upon him by the Jepanese autho-
rities against his will. He also claims that he had his passport
renewed at theAmerican Consul ate in Mukden in 1939 and that he
registered as an American citizen at the American Consulate in
Yokohama in September 1941.
5C

ACPPET
MEMORANDUM
Re:
Post Surrender Japanese Activities in China
A report received from Shanghat, dated 26 January 1946, and evaluated
C-2 states that The National Salvatron and Resurreotion of the Motherland
Center is an outgrowth of "The Ideological Center," an organization established
in June 1945 at Shanghai by certain Japanese individuals who realized that the
defeat of Japan was approaching. The general situation prevailing at that
time made it imperative that the members work in absolute secrecy for any hint
or suggestion about the possibility of defeat would lead to arrest by the gen-
darmerie. This fact severely restricted membership and the individuals who
ostablished "The Ideological Center" were closely and intimately connected among
themselves.
The sudden end of the Pacific war caught the "Center" while it was still
engaged in the process of preliminary organization. The actual conditions and
the terms of surrender imposed upon Japan were astoundingly different from what
was expected. The "Center" was confronted with the immediate problem of speed
rnd drastic readjustment of its program or of quitting entirely. It is interesting
to note that until the surrender none of these Japanese believed Manohuria,
Korea and Formosa would be taken away and thet all Jap civilians in China would
be shipped back to Japan together with he Japanese military personnel. The
"Center" chose to continue in existence and this decision was made possible by
the fact that even the most chauvinistic and reactionary army and navy circles
were willing to support it. Consequently, a new program was set up and the
organization assumed the new name under which it is now functioning.
Program:
Phe Program of "The Tdeological Senter" bofore the termination of the
war was based on the following principles:
1. Japan's defst should be explained es a Divine Trial of the whol•
Tapanes, Race on the road to eventual achievement of the lofty aims of HAko-
ISuIl. Through the temporary misfortunes and difficulties of the Post Surrender
Period the Japanese would emerge as the fully qualified conquerors and rulers
of East Asia. This Divine Trial was necessary beceuse many Japanese were not
worthy of these high aims. They lacked unity, perseverance and the spirit of
self-sacrifice and complete devotion to the Emperor.
2. Irrospective of the terms of the surrender it was proposed to main-
tain and strengthen the unity of purpose of all the Japanese nationals who would
have to stay in China after the conclusion of the peace treaty and to guide the
Japanese overseas organizations for the maintenance of dignity as well as to use
properly and effectively their material and spiritual assets for the ultimate
glory of the Japanese Empire.
S. In order to meet any possible situation which might arise after the
conclusion of peace with the Allies it was decided to use the "Idealogical
Center" as the nucleus for a secret underground organization devoted to the pro-
motion of the cause of reater Japen in all fields of economic and political
activity by ussuning fir: leadership over all local Japanese social and private
associations and gradually extending its influence to unify the whole Japanese
community in Shanghai.
SECRET

=== Page 288 === (confidence 0.97)
SECRET
octnet
-2-
4. Without waiting for future developments it was also decided to
commence at once the work of establishing secret filial organs whioh would
permit the start of necessary activities immediately after the conclusion of
peace and in aocordance with the actual requirements.
After the surrender of Japan and the subsequent broadening of the
scope of the activities of the organization a new fundamental ooncept was a-
dopted as the basis for a program of notivity. This concept is reported to be:
Japan had not been occupied by the enemy when the Emperor had com-
manded the surrender of ell the armed forces nor had the Japanese Army been
beaten in actual combat with the enemy and defeated militarily. Thus the
Emperor's comnand for surrender was caused by other reasons whioh do not cast
a shadow upon the honor of the Japanese Army. These reasons were: First and
foremost, the use by the allies of the terrible new invention called the atomio
bomb which murders horrible numbers of non-combatant women and children with-
out any direct effect upon the fighting forces and therefore which must be
termed as an atrocity and a crime against humanity and civilization. Secondly,
the treachery of the Soviet Union which had occurred simultaneously with the
7
atomic bomb made his Majesty in his profound concern over the sufferings of
the innocent population graciously decide to assume the full responsibility
for surrerdering upon his august and divine person. This latter fact must be
imprinted in the hearts of all true Japanese and it must be made absolutely
clean to them that their first and foremost sacred duty before their Emperor,
their country and posterity is to redeem his Ma iesty's personal sacrifice by
concentrating their whole energy, effort and life for the task of redemption.
This must be attained at any price and through any conceivable means. Immortal
glory would crown the achievement of this aim.
Members of Organization
The following have been identified as members of the former
"Ideological Center" and ere still members of the "National Salvation and
Resurrection of the Motherland Center:"
K. MORIYAMA - is chairman of the group. He wes e former High
Adviser to the Japanese Ambassador in Washington (1923-25). Later he was re-
tired from official service. His address as of 1 November 1945 is 104-11
Scott Road.
T. HAYASHI - is an ex-naval officer who was retired in 1918. He has
been engaged as a small coal merchant. And since his brother's death he has
acted as the sole proprietor. His home and business address as of 1 November
1945 is 159 Boone Road.
Dr. Arita YOSHIDA - was formerly on the staff of WASEDA University.
His address is unknown. Formerly he lived in Hangchow in the Japanese Medical
College Faculty House.
Activities
Since the adoption of their new fundamental concep the activities
of the organization have been expanded and various branches o. the "National
Salvation and Resurrection of the Motherland Center" are using all available
methods and resources for the attainment of their aims. "Emergency" measures
are devised on the spot according to necessity and may include sabotage,

SEGRET
anti-foreign propaganda and assistance to all nationalistic patriotio political
movements of natives in Asiatic countries. Some of the activities of the "Center"
are given below.
1. Asiatic Communism - This tern was only recently invented by the
Japanese "Ideologists" to denote the difference between Occidental and Oriental
economic, social rnd cultural conditions which makes "European Communism" as
evolved in Russia absolutely unadoptable in Asia. This new Japanese politioal
invention, it is reported, has been very efficiently carried throughout the
occupied territories in China and the Pecific regions ever since the surrender.
This campaign is designed to increase the disorder in China and other Asiatic
countries and to obstruct Allied efforts to stabilize the situation and thus
directly or indirectly assist Japan in regaining its political importance as the
stabilizing power in Asia and as the only dependable power in the Far East which
is Anti-Communistic and which might later be used to suppress the Communists.
2. Arms Smuggling - Another "Emergency" measure is the smuggling of arms
and ammunition to dissident Chinese groups in Shanghai. Some agencies inoluding
those run by Koreans with extensive Japanese connections were known to have ef-
fected the transfer of new Japanese machine guns and other weapons. Several
foreigners were also used to effect the transfer of arms on a smaller scale.
Those persons identified in this connectior are:
N. Shturtz RoMOSLAVSKy - a large arms and ammunition dealer during
the war who worked with SAITO, Chief of the Foreign Affairs Section of the puppet
police.
A report, deted 1 November 1945, and evaluated C-3 states that
ROMOSLAVSKY was arrested in the spring of 1942 by the Frenoh Police for selling
to a Japanese a police pistol belonging to A. M. GLUCk (See comnents on GLUCK
below). He was imprisoned fer two weeks.
Adrian KASHIN - Three reports dated Oct. 3, Nov. 29, Nov. 30, 1945
and evaluated as F-2, C-3 and B-2 report that KASIIN resides at 803 Bubbling
Well Road, Apt. 8. He has worked for the Japanese Gendarmerie, Army and Navy
and has participated in much swindling and blackrailing on behalf of these
services. Ie was arrested for selling false shares of the Ewo Cotton Mills
but the Jap Gendarmerie secured his release. The apartment in which he is
living was given to him by the Gendarmerie when the owner was placed in a con-
centration camp. In September 1945, he was in contact with Colonel AMAYA of
the Japanese Military Mission and also with O. A. K. WITTINSKY, ex-D.S.I. of
the Shanghei Municipal Police. He is also reported to be the closest friend
of a man named HOVAlS for whom he has worked.
Two reports, dated 1 November 1945 and 30 November 1945, and evaluated
as C-3 and B-2 state that IOVAIS had been imprisoned for 15 years for murder
when the war broke out. The night the war started he was released by the
Japanese. He then began to work for the Japanese Havy and became the No. 1
Naval Intelligence Agents' Supervisor. By his direction many people were re-
ported to have been imprisoned and killed. Before the war he is reported to
have worked for the American Secret Service. It is also reported that this
fact was known to the Japanese.
M. JIMBO - Also of the firm of J. H. Way.
Arkady Michailovitch GLucK and many Koreans, Chinese, Portuguese, etc.
The names of the units and groups receiving these arms are under investigation.

SECRET
A report, dated 1 November 1945, and evaluated C-3 states that
prior to the civil war GLUCK was employed by agents of the U. S. Naval
Attaches office in Shanghai. After the outbreak of the war he assisted the
Japanese in locating radio receiving and sending sets owned by Allied nationals.
He is a radio expert and a member of the Amerioan International Radio Amateurs'
Association. He was arrested in 1943 and again in 1944 for partioipation in
burglary and received varied terms of imprisonment. At present he is displaying
large sums of money and is boastinf that he has resumed his old contacts with
the American Intelligence Service receiving especially liberal pay.
3. Japanese Communist Pretenders - The "Center" is carrying but aprogram
designed to penetrate the Soviet Union. Japanese who pretend to be Japanese
Communists are applyirg confidentially for Soviet papers. One example of such
an attempt it be seen in the case of a certain T. koliArsU who is known to
the Chinese as Siao PEI. He is a notorious Gendarmerie Sergeant who was mys-
teriously discharged and is now an innocent "civilian resident". He is applying
for Soviet papers. KoYATsl has succeeded in getting three necessary guarantors
from among, local bonafide Soviet citizens. He has also applied for a Chinese
Gendarmerie Pass through other channels to protect himself in any "misunderstanding."
4. Japanese Propapanda - The Center is conducting vigorous propaganda
amonp their own netionals to the effect that the U. S. is peparing for a war with
the Soviet Union over the luestion of world control. This World War TII, they
state, must occur within the next two or three years (i.e., before giving time
to Russia to develcp the atonic bonh end other modern secret weapons), and that
before this third World War has cormenced the U. S. is frantically trying to
reeducate the Japarese so that Japan can serve as the base of operations of the
American Air Forces carrying atomic bombs against the Soviet Union. When World
war II breaks, Japan will have her chance to make a good bargain with the
U.S.A. at the expense of Soviet Russia.
5. Japanese Special Agents in Foreign Affairs Section of Chinese
Organizations - It is deserving of attention from the point of view of the above-
descrihed secret Japanese activities that the newly established headquarters of the
Chinese 25th Division located in the Medhurst Building in Bubbling Well Road,
has engaged several Japanese Secret Agents for supplying the Chinese Command
with information about the Soviet, Chinese Communists and the foreigners. The
term "foreigners" includes the British, the French and the American Sections.
Among the newly enrolled Japanese Secret Agents is a former official of the
Japanese Military Mission, K. uROkI, wio is residing at present in 20l Quinsan
Road. KiRoki is working on the Soviets.
A report, dated 1 November 1945, and evaluated B-2 states that
KURokI is a former official of the Jepanese Military Mission in charge of the
Russian Section. He was chief editor and publisher of a Japanese undercover
newspaper "Novae Vienia" (New Times) written in Russian end now circulated under
a new name "SHANGHAISKIE WOVOSTI" (Shanghai Hews). He has been attempting to
sell large quentities of Japanese Military stores and has tried to purchase a
Swedish or Portuguese passport.

=== Page 292 === (confidence 0.99)
SEGRET
MEMORANDUM
Re:
Princess Sumair of Patiala
Princess Sumair, one of the more notorious members of
Shanghai's cafe society, a former associate ofprominent Nazis and
high-ranking Japanese and presently a favorite companion of American
military personnel and rich Shanghailanders is the wife of an American
citizen of Japanese ancestry, Morihiko TAKAMI. TAKAMI, who played the
black market during the war and who served as an informer for Japanese
Naval Intelligence, is now in jail in Tokyo. The Princess, however,
is not disconsolate. She is said to be currently the mistress of a
well-known American war correspondent. She is making money on the side
by selling property she loesn't own and by blackmailing her past lovers.
She is also getting money from Japanese on the pretext that her husband,
who, according to her is a major in the U. S. Intelligence Service, can
get it into Japan.
The Princess - her clain to that title is not too well es-
tablished - first entered the worlu of high society in 1938 in Paris,
as an "intimate of Madame Schiapparelli". According to one report,
she is the wife of a Sikh from Patiala named Sumair. According to
another, she is the wife of a Maharujah with two children by him.
According to a third and more likely report, she is the illegitimate
daughter of the Maharaja of Patiala. On hearing of her existence, the
latter is renorted to have said, "She may very well be my daughter.
I have twenty-three of them." Mhatever her antecedents, the Princess
seems to have been accepted by Parisian society and later by the
Shanghai smart set.
In 1940, Trincess Sunair arrived in Shanghai for the second
time with plenty of money to spend and prominent and wealthy men like
Sasson to spend it with. (ler first visit had gone unnoticed and was
evidently of a less spectacular character.) Her friends - and her
friendships were eldom of a Platonic nature - were many, both men and
women. Helen Bascha, now escorted everywhere by American military men,
an Italian boy friend for whom she bought a motor car, her secretary
Olga, and Roger Pierrard, rich Frenchman not accepted by the local
French colony, all helped to make Princess Sumair's first free spending
years in Shangnai pass pleasuntly. Eventually, her money ran out but
she continued to live as usual on money obtained by borrowing and by
blackmailing her friend M. Pierrard. But the girl friends multiplied
and all sources of credit, including Pierrard, ran out. The Princess
began to move from place to place, one step ahead of the numerous bill
collectors.
In 1942, the Princess met Morihiko TAKAMI and moved into
his appartment. But she gave up neither her men nor her women friends.
In 1943, TAKAMI married the Princess and their house on Route Coulty
became a center of the champagne-drinking, caviar-eating crowd. Most
